========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, thank you for seeing me. I... well, I'm here because things at work have just gotten completely out of hand, and I don't know what else to do. I work as a bookkeeping clerk, right? So I deal with numbers and spreadsheets all day. And lately—well, honestly it's been building up for a while, but it's just so bad now—I can't stop going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations again and again. 

I mean, I'll finish a ledger, and I know I did it right. I'll even check the math, and it balances perfectly. But then this feeling hits me, just this overwhelming dread, and I have to go back and check it again. And then I'll check it again. And I'll think, "Okay, that's the last time, I'm going to close the file," but my hand just won't click the mouse to close it, because my brain is screaming at me that if I don't check it one more time, I've missed a decimal point or transposed a number, and the whole company's finances are going to be ruined, and it'll be entirely my fault. 

It's gotten to the point where I'm staying at work for hours and hours after everyone else leaves. Like, the office is dark, the cleaning crew has already come and gone, and I'm just sitting there at my desk, staring at a screen I've already reviewed twenty times. I just... I feel like something terrible is going to happen if I don't do it just one more time, and I can't stop myself, no matter how exhausted I am.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, outside of work? Um... no, not really. It's funny, you'd think I would, right? Like if I'm so obsessed with the numbers at work, you'd think I'd be exactly the same way with my own bank account or my bills at home. But honestly, I just... I don't. When it comes to my own stuff, I just pay my electric bill or whatever and I don't even think twice about it. I might glance at my bank balance on my phone, but I'm not sitting there for three hours making sure the math adds up. 

It's really just the work stuff. And I've thought about that a lot, actually. Like, why is it only at the office? I think maybe it's because... well, at home, if I mess up my own checking account, it's just me, you know? Like, I bounce a check, I get a fee, whatever. It's my problem. But at work, I'm handling thousands and thousands of dollars that belong to the company, and we have vendors relying on us, and payroll, and if I mess that up, it's not just a little overdraft fee. It's like... people's livelihoods. And that weight, that responsibility, it just sits on my chest all day. So when I go home, I'm just so mentally drained from checking the work spreadsheets that I don't even have the energy to care about my own mail. I just leave it on the kitchen counter and go to sleep. So, yeah, no, it doesn't really happen at home. Just work. Just... constantly at work.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, outside of work? Not really, no. Like I said, it really is just... it all comes back to the office. But even when I'm not physically sitting there doing the checking, the thoughts about it just... they don't stop. I mean, the ideas that pop into my head are just so loud, and they follow me everywhere. 

So, for example, I'll be driving home, or I'll be sitting on my couch trying to watch a show, and even after I *know* the numbers match—like, I literally saw the zero balance, I saw the green checkmark in the software, I know I did the math right—this thought just keeps jumping in. It just completely invades my head that I missed one hidden mistake somewhere deep in the payroll file, or maybe in the quarterly taxes. 

And it's not just a passing "oh, I hope I did that right" kind of thought. It's this whole terrifying scenario playing out. I start picturing that somebody's check won't go through because of me. Like, maybe one of the warehouse guys won't be able to pay his rent because I transposed a digit on his direct deposit, or the firm will get in massive trouble with the IRS over a tax error and we'll get audited and it will all trace back to my desk and I'll be the reason the whole business goes under. 

I try so, so hard to push it away. I'll literally shake my head, or I'll turn up the radio in my car to drown it out, and I'll tell myself, "You checked it, you checked it twenty times, it's fine." But it comes right back. It comes right back every single time, usually worse than before. It's like... it's like I can't trust my own eyes anymore. I'll remember seeing the right number on the screen, but my brain tells me, "Are you sure? Are you really sure you saw a five and not a six?" And then I'm just spiraling again, thinking about how I'm going to ruin someone's life over a payroll glitch. 

So, yeah, the thoughts are just... they're completely consumed by that. I don't really have space in my brain to worry about anything else, like getting sick or whatever, because that one fear about the accounts is just taking up all the room, if that makes sense. It's just exhausting. I can't even enjoy a normal evening at home without thinking about tax penalties and ruined paychecks.

Like, maybe one of the warehouse guys won't be able to pay his rent because I transposed a digit on his direct deposit, or the firm will get in massive trouble with the IRS over a tax error and we'll get audited and it will all trace back to my desk and I'll be the reason the whole business goes under. \n\nI try so, so hard to push it away. I'll literally shake my head, or I'll turn up the radio in my car to drown it out, and I'll tell myself, \"You checked it, you checked it twenty times, it's fine.\" But it comes right back. It comes right back every single time, usually worse than before. It's like... it's like I can't trust my own eyes anymore. I'll remember seeing the right number on the screen, but my brain tells me, \"Are you sure? Are you really sure you saw a five and not a six?\" And then I'm just spiraling again, thinking about how I'm going to ruin someone's life over a payroll glitch. \n\nSo, yeah, the thoughts are just... they're completely consumed by that. I don't really have space in my brain to worry about anything else, like getting sick or whatever, because that one fear about the accounts is just taking up all the room, if that makes sense. It's just exhausting. I can't even enjoy a normal evening at home without thinking about tax penalties and ruined paychecks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes! Exactly. That is exactly what it feels like. It’s like... if I don't do it, if I just pack up my bag and walk out the door at five o'clock like a normal person, then whatever disaster is waiting to happen is going to happen, and it will be entirely my fault because I didn't stop it. 

I know I brought this up when we first started talking, but that's honestly the whole reason I'm here. I literally cannot stop going back over the exact same spreadsheets and the exact same calculations again and again. I'm staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves, just sitting there by myself in the dark office, and it's because checking the numbers is the only thing that feels like it's keeping the company safe. 

If I just look at it one more time, I can prevent the ruined paychecks. I can prevent the tax audit. But if I don't... if I try to just close the Excel file and go home, this massive wave of panic hits me, telling me that because I didn't check it that one last time, the fatal mistake is in there. By checking it, it feels like I'm actively stopping the bad things from happening. It’s almost like it resets the danger or something, you know? Like, "Okay, I checked it, the math is right, we're safe." 

But then five minutes later, my brain says, "Are you sure? What if you looked away for a second and missed a negative sign?" And then I have to do it all over again to prevent the disaster all over again. It’s just this endless, exhausting loop where checking is the only way to protect everyone from my own potential mistakes. Even when I've already checked them twenty times, if I don't do it just one more time... I just feel like something terrible will happen, the whole place will go under, and it will be one hundred percent my fault. I just can't live with that kind of guilt, so I keep checking. I just keep checking.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you for seeing me. I... well, I'm here because things at work have just gotten completely out of hand, and I don't know what else to do. I work as a bookkeeping clerk, right? So I deal with numbers and spreadsheets all day. And lately—well, honestly it's been building up for a while, but it's just so bad now—I can't stop going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations again and again. \n\nI mean, I'll finish a ledger, and I know I did it right. I'll even check the math, and it balances perfectly. But then this feeling hits me, just this overwhelming dread, and I have to go back and check it again. And then I'll check it again. And I'll think, \"Okay, that's the last time, I'm going to close the file,\" but my hand just won't click the mouse to close it, because my brain is screaming at me that if I don't check it one more time, I've missed a decimal point or transposed a number, and the whole company's finances are going to be ruined, and it'll be entirely my fault. \n\nIt's gotten to the point where I'm staying at work for hours and hours after everyone else leaves. Like, the office is dark, the cleaning crew has already come and gone, and I'm just sitting there at my desk, staring at a screen I've already reviewed twenty times. I just... I feel like something terrible is going to happen if I don't do it just one more time, and I can't stop myself, no matter how exhausted I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating these checking behaviors outside of your work, like with personal finances or other tasks at home?\n\nPatient: I mean, outside of work? Um... no, not really. It's funny, you'd think I would, right? Like if I'm so obsessed with the numbers at work, you'd think I'd be exactly the same way with my own bank account or my bills at home. But honestly, I just... I don't. When it comes to my own stuff, I just pay my electric bill or whatever and I don't even think twice about it. I might glance at my bank balance on my phone, but I'm not sitting there for three hours making sure the math adds up. \n\nIt's really just the work stuff. And I've thought about that a lot, actually. Like, why is it only at the office? I think maybe it's because... well, at home, if I mess up my own checking account, it's just me, you know? Like, I bounce a check, I get a fee, whatever. It's my problem. It's like... people's livelihoods. And that weight, that responsibility, it just sits on my chest all day. So when I go home, I'm just so mentally drained from checking the work spreadsheets that I don't even have the energy to care about my own mail. I just leave it on the kitchen counter and go to sleep. So, yeah, no, it doesn't really happen at home. Just work. Just... constantly at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts or ideas that bother you outside of your concerns about work and the checking behaviors you've described?\n\nPatient: Um, outside of work? I mean, the ideas that pop into my head are just so loud, and they follow me everywhere. \n\nSo, for example, I'll be driving home, or I'll be sitting on my couch trying to watch a show, and even after I *know* the numbers match—like, I literally saw the zero balance, I saw the green checkmark in the software, I know I did the math right—this thought just keeps jumping in. It just completely invades my head that I missed one hidden mistake somewhere deep in the payroll file, or maybe in the quarterly taxes. \n\nAnd it's not just a passing \"oh, I hope I did that right\" kind of thought. It's this whole terrifying scenario playing out. I start picturing that somebody's check won't go through because of me. Like, maybe one of the warehouse guys won't be able to pay his rent because I transposed a digit on his direct deposit, or the firm will get in massive trouble with the IRS over a tax error and we'll get audited and it will all trace back to my desk and I'll be the reason the whole business goes under. \n\nI try so, so hard to push it away. I'll literally shake my head, or I'll turn up the radio in my car to drown it out, and I'll tell myself, \"You checked it, you checked it twenty times, it's fine.\" But it comes right back. It comes right back every single time, usually worse than before. It's like... it's like I can't trust my own eyes anymore. I'll remember seeing the right number on the screen, but my brain tells me, \"Are you sure? I don't really have space in my brain to worry about anything else, like getting sick or whatever, because that one fear about the accounts is just taking up all the room, if that makes sense. It's just exhausting. I can't even enjoy a normal evening at home without thinking about tax penalties and ruined paychecks.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the spreadsheets again and again is what prevents the bad things you worry about, like the company's finances being ruined, from actually happening?\n\nPatient: Yes! Exactly. That is exactly what it feels like. It’s like... if I don't do it, if I just pack up my bag and walk out the door at five o'clock like a normal person, then whatever disaster is waiting to happen is going to happen, and it will be entirely my fault because I didn't stop it. \n\nI know I brought this up when we first started talking, but that's honestly the whole reason I'm here. I literally cannot stop going back over the exact same spreadsheets and the exact same calculations again and again. I'm staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves, just sitting there by myself in the dark office, and it's because checking the numbers is the only thing that feels like it's keeping the company safe. \n\nIf I just look at it one more time, I can prevent the ruined paychecks. I can prevent the tax audit. Like, \"Okay, I checked it, the math is right, we're safe.\" \n\nBut then five minutes later, my brain says, \"Are you sure? What if you looked away for a second and missed a negative sign?\" And then I have to do it all over again to prevent the disaster all over again. It’s just this endless, exhausting loop where checking is the only way to protect everyone from my own potential mistakes. Even when I've already checked them twenty times, if I don't do it just one more time... I just feel like something terrible will happen, the whole place will go under, and it will be one hundred percent my fault. I just can't live with that kind of guilt, so I keep checking. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think... I mean, it's really a bit of both? But it definitely feels like I'm forced. Like I was just saying a second ago, the whole reason I even came to this clinic in the first place is because I literally cannot stop going back over those same exact spreadsheets and calculations again and again. It's not like I want to be doing it, you know? I don't want to be sitting there at eight o'clock at night when my shift ended at five. 

But it's this overwhelming, crushing feeling that I absolutely have to do it. Like there's an actual physical weight pushing me back down into my desk chair. If I stand up, grab my purse, and try to walk toward the elevator, my heart just starts pounding out of my chest, and my brain is screaming at me that if I don't do it just one more time, something terrible is going to happen and it will be completely my fault. So I feel forced by that fear, if that makes sense. 

But then... when I do sit back down, and I pull the file back up, and I run the numbers again, tracing my finger down the monitor to make sure every single decimal point is exactly where it should be... I do get this tiny, split-second of relief. It's like, "Okay, thank God, it's fine, I didn't ruin anyone's life today." My shoulders drop, I can finally take a real breath. 

The problem is, that feeling just doesn't last. It evaporates almost instantly. Before I can even get my coat on, the doubt creeps back in. My brain says, "Wait, did you actually check the third tab, or did you just skim it?" And then I'm forced to sit down and do it all over again. So I guess I'm chasing that relief, but I only have to chase it because I feel completely held hostage by the thought that if I don't check, everything falls apart and it's on my shoulders. That's why I'm staying at work for hours after everyone leaves, even when I already checked everything twenty times. I'm just trapped in this cycle of being terrified, checking, feeling okay for two seconds, and then being terrified all over again.

Like, the office is dark, the cleaning crew has already come and gone, and I'm just sitting there at my desk, staring at a screen I've already reviewed twenty times. I just... I feel like something terrible is going to happen if I don't do it just one more time, and I can't stop myself, no matter how exhausted I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating these checking behaviors outside of your work, like with personal finances or other tasks at home?\n\nPatient: I mean, outside of work? Um... no, not really. It's funny, you'd think I would, right? Like if I'm so obsessed with the numbers at work, you'd think I'd be exactly the same way with my own bank account or my bills at home. But honestly, I just... I don't. When it comes to my own stuff, I just pay my electric bill or whatever and I don't even think twice about it. I might glance at my bank balance on my phone, but I'm not sitting there for three hours making sure the math adds up. \n\nIt's really just the work stuff. And I've thought about that a lot, actually. Like, why is it only at the office? I think maybe it's because... well, at home, if I mess up my own checking account, it's just me, you know? Like, I bounce a check, I get a fee, whatever. It's my problem. But at work, I'm handling thousands and thousands of dollars that belong to the company, and we have vendors relying on us, and payroll, and if I mess that up, it's not just a little overdraft fee. It's like... people's livelihoods. And that weight, that responsibility, it just sits on my chest all day. So when I go home, I'm just so mentally drained from checking the work spreadsheets that I don't even have the energy to care about my own mail. I just leave it on the kitchen counter and go to sleep. So, yeah, no, it doesn't really happen at home. Just work. Just... constantly at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts or ideas that bother you outside of your concerns about work and the checking behaviors you've described?\n\nPatient: Um, outside of work? Not really, no. Like I said, it really is just... it all comes back to the office. But even when I'm not physically sitting there doing the checking, the thoughts about it just... they don't stop. I mean, the ideas that pop into my head are just so loud, and they follow me everywhere. \n\nSo, for example, I'll be driving home, or I'll be sitting on my couch trying to watch a show, and even after I *know* the numbers match—like, I literally saw the zero balance, I saw the green checkmark in the software, I know I did the math right—this thought just keeps jumping in. It just completely invades my head that I missed one hidden mistake somewhere deep in the payroll file, or maybe in the quarterly taxes. \n\nAnd it's not just a passing \"oh, I hope I did that right\" kind of thought. It's this whole terrifying scenario playing out. I start picturing that somebody's check won't go through because of me. Like, maybe one of the warehouse guys won't be able to pay his rent because I transposed a digit on his direct deposit, or the firm will get in massive trouble with the IRS over a tax error and we'll get audited and it will all trace back to my desk and I'll be the reason the whole business goes under. \n\nI try so, so hard to push it away. I'll literally shake my head, or I'll turn up the radio in my car to drown it out, and I'll tell myself, \"You checked it, you checked it twenty times, it's fine.\" But it comes right back. It comes right back every single time, usually worse than before. It's like... it's like I can't trust my own eyes anymore. I'll remember seeing the right number on the screen, but my brain tells me, \"Are you sure? Are you really sure you saw a five and not a six?\" And then I'm just spiraling again, thinking about how I'm going to ruin someone's life over a payroll glitch. \n\nSo, yeah, the thoughts are just... they're completely consumed by that. I don't really have space in my brain to worry about anything else, like getting sick or whatever, because that one fear about the accounts is just taking up all the room, if that makes sense. It's just exhausting. I can't even enjoy a normal evening at home without thinking about tax penalties and ruined paychecks.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the spreadsheets again and again is what prevents the bad things you worry about, like the company's finances being ruined, from actually happening?\n\nPatient: Yes! Exactly. That is exactly what it feels like. It’s like... if I don't do it, if I just pack up my bag and walk out the door at five o'clock like a normal person, then whatever disaster is waiting to happen is going to happen, and it will be entirely my fault because I didn't stop it. \n\nI know I brought this up when we first started talking, but that's honestly the whole reason I'm here. I literally cannot stop going back over the exact same spreadsheets and the exact same calculations again and again. I'm staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves, just sitting there by myself in the dark office, and it's because checking the numbers is the only thing that feels like it's keeping the company safe. \n\nIf I just look at it one more time, I can prevent the ruined paychecks. I can prevent the tax audit. But if I don't... if I try to just close the Excel file and go home, this massive wave of panic hits me, telling me that because I didn't check it that one last time, the fatal mistake is in there. By checking it, it feels like I'm actively stopping the bad things from happening. It’s almost like it resets the danger or something, you know? Like, \"Okay, I checked it, the math is right, we're safe.\" \n\nBut then five minutes later, my brain says, \"Are you sure? What if you looked away for a second and missed a negative sign?\" And then I have to do it all over again to prevent the disaster all over again. It’s just this endless, exhausting loop where checking is the only way to protect everyone from my own potential mistakes. Even when I've already checked them twenty times, if I don't do it just one more time... I just feel like something terrible will happen, the whole place will go under, and it will be one hundred percent my fault. I just can't live with that kind of guilt, so I keep checking. I just keep checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check the spreadsheets again and again because you feel forced to, or is it more because you're trying to achieve a certain feeling, like relief?\n\nPatient: I think... I mean, it's really a bit of both? But it definitely feels like I'm forced. Like I was just saying a second ago, the whole reason I even came to this clinic in the first place is because I literally cannot stop going back over those same exact spreadsheets and calculations again and again. It's not like I want to be doing it, you know? I don't want to be sitting there at eight o'clock at night when my shift ended at five. \n\nBut it's this overwhelming, crushing feeling that I absolutely have to do it. Like there's an actual physical weight pushing me back down into my desk chair. If I stand up, grab my purse, and try to walk toward the elevator, my heart just starts pounding out of my chest, and my brain is screaming at me that if I don't do it just one more time, something terrible is going to happen and it will be completely my fault. So I feel forced by that fear, if that makes sense. \n\nBut then... when I do sit back down, and I pull the file back up, and I run the numbers again, tracing my finger down the monitor to make sure every single decimal point is exactly where it should be... I do get this tiny, split-second of relief. It's like, \"Okay, thank God, it's fine, I didn't ruin anyone's life today.\" My shoulders drop, I can finally take a real breath. \n\nThe problem is, that feeling just doesn't last. It evaporates almost instantly. Before I can even get my coat on, the doubt creeps back in. My brain says, \"Wait, did you actually check the third tab, or did you just skim it?\" And then I'm forced to sit down and do it all over again. So I guess I'm chasing that relief, but I only have to chase it because I feel completely held hostage by the thought that if I don't check, everything falls apart and it's on my shoulders. That's why I'm staying at work for hours after everyone leaves, even when I already checked everything twenty times. I'm just trapped in this cycle of being terrified, checking, feeling okay for two seconds, and then being terrified all over again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Not really, no. Like I said, it really is just... it all comes back to the office. But even when I'm not physically sitting there doing the checking, the thoughts about it just... they don't stop. I mean, the ideas that pop into my head are just so loud, and they follow me everywhere. \n\nSo, for example, I'll be driving home, or I'll be sitting on my couch trying to watch a show, and even after I *know* the numbers match—like, I literally saw the zero balance, I saw the green checkmark in the software, I know I did the math right—this thought just keeps jumping in. It just completely invades my head that I missed one hidden mistake somewhere deep in the payroll file, or maybe in the quarterly taxes. \n\nAnd it's not just a passing \"oh, I hope I did that right\" kind of thought. It's this whole terrifying scenario playing out. I start picturing that somebody's check won't go through because of me. Like, maybe one of the warehouse guys won't be able to pay his rent because I transposed a digit on his direct deposit, or the firm will get in massive trouble with the IRS over a tax error and we'll get audited and it will all trace back to my desk and I'll be the reason the whole business goes under. \n\nI try so, so hard to push it away. I'll literally shake my head, or I'll turn up the radio in my car to drown it out, and I'll tell myself, \"You checked it, you checked it twenty times, it's fine.\" But it comes right back. It comes right back every single time, usually worse than before. It's like... it's like I can't trust my own eyes anymore. I'll remember seeing the right number on the screen, but my brain tells me, \"Are you sure? Are you really sure you saw a five and not a six?\" And then I'm just spiraling again, thinking about how I'm going to ruin someone's life over a payroll glitch. \n\nSo, yeah, the thoughts are just... they're completely consumed by that. I don't really have space in my brain to worry about anything else, like getting sick or whatever, because that one fear about the accounts is just taking up all the room, if that makes sense. It's just exhausting. I can't even enjoy a normal evening at home without thinking about tax penalties and ruined paychecks.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the spreadsheets again and again is what prevents the bad things you worry about, like the company's finances being ruined, from actually happening?\n\nPatient: Yes! Exactly. That is exactly what it feels like. It’s like... if I don't do it, if I just pack up my bag and walk out the door at five o'clock like a normal person, then whatever disaster is waiting to happen is going to happen, and it will be entirely my fault because I didn't stop it. \n\nI know I brought this up when we first started talking, but that's honestly the whole reason I'm here. I literally cannot stop going back over the exact same spreadsheets and the exact same calculations again and again. I'm staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves, just sitting there by myself in the dark office, and it's because checking the numbers is the only thing that feels like it's keeping the company safe. \n\nIf I just look at it one more time, I can prevent the ruined paychecks. I can prevent the tax audit. But if I don't... if I try to just close the Excel file and go home, this massive wave of panic hits me, telling me that because I didn't check it that one last time, the fatal mistake is in there. By checking it, it feels like I'm actively stopping the bad things from happening. It’s almost like it resets the danger or something, you know? Like, \"Okay, I checked it, the math is right, we're safe.\" \n\nBut then five minutes later, my brain says, \"Are you sure? What if you looked away for a second and missed a negative sign?\" And then I have to do it all over again to prevent the disaster all over again. It’s just this endless, exhausting loop where checking is the only way to protect everyone from my own potential mistakes. Even when I've already checked them twenty times, if I don't do it just one more time... I just feel like something terrible will happen, the whole place will go under, and it will be one hundred percent my fault. I just can't live with that kind of guilt, so I keep checking. I just keep checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check the spreadsheets again and again because you feel forced to, or is it more because you're trying to achieve a certain feeling, like relief?\n\nPatient: I think... I mean, it's really a bit of both? But it definitely feels like I'm forced. Like I was just saying a second ago, the whole reason I even came to this clinic in the first place is because I literally cannot stop going back over those same exact spreadsheets and calculations again and again. It's not like I want to be doing it, you know? I don't want to be sitting there at eight o'clock at night when my shift ended at five. \n\nBut it's this overwhelming, crushing feeling that I absolutely have to do it. Like there's an actual physical weight pushing me back down into my desk chair. If I stand up, grab my purse, and try to walk toward the elevator, my heart just starts pounding out of my chest, and my brain is screaming at me that if I don't do it just one more time, something terrible is going to happen and it will be completely my fault. So I feel forced by that fear, if that makes sense. \n\nBut then... when I do sit back down, and I pull the file back up, and I run the numbers again, tracing my finger down the monitor to make sure every single decimal point is exactly where it should be... I do get this tiny, split-second of relief. It's like, \"Okay, thank God, it's fine, I didn't ruin anyone's life today.\" My shoulders drop, I can finally take a real breath. \n\nThe problem is, that feeling just doesn't last. It evaporates almost instantly. Before I can even get my coat on, the doubt creeps back in. My brain says, \"Wait, did you actually check the third tab, or did you just skim it?\" And then I'm forced to sit down and do it all over again. So I guess I'm chasing that relief, but I only have to chase it because I feel completely held hostage by the thought that if I don't check, everything falls apart and it's on my shoulders. That's why I'm staying at work for hours after everyone leaves, even when I already checked everything twenty times. I'm just trapped in this cycle of being terrified, checking, feeling okay for two seconds, and then being terrified all over again.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like the numbers or the way the spreadsheets are organized need to be a certain way or look a certain way for you to feel okay about them?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, like... organized a certain way? Or looking a certain way? No, not really. I mean, the spreadsheets we use are mostly just standard templates anyway. The software we use just exports everything into these basic, you know, standard Excel grids, and I really don't care about the formatting at all. Like, I don't care if the columns are perfectly spaced out, or if the font is Arial instead of Calibri, or if the headers are highlighted in yellow or something. I know some of the other clerks at the office get really particular about that stuff, like they want everything to look super neat and color-coded and perfectly aligned before they save the file, but that doesn't bother me even a little bit. 

For me, it is one hundred percent just about the actual data. It's not about how the numbers look on the screen, it's about what they *mean*. Like I was saying before, my whole thing is just... is the math right? Did I type a five instead of a two? Did I put a decimal in the wrong spot? Because that's the stuff that actually ruins things. A messy spreadsheet isn't going to cause the IRS to audit us, but a wrong number absolutely will. 

So, yeah, I'm not sitting there for hours in the dark rearranging the rows to make them look pretty or balanced or whatever. I'm just staring at the exact same cell, reading the value over and over and over again, terrified that my eyes are playing tricks on me and I missed a negative sign. If I knew for an absolute fact that the math was perfect, it could be the ugliest, most disorganized spreadsheet in the entire world and I wouldn't care at all. It's just that fear of making a fatal mistake that I can't let go of, you know? It always just comes back to whether or not I'm going to be the reason someone's paycheck bounces.

It's not like I want to be doing it, you know? I don't want to be sitting there at eight o'clock at night when my shift ended at five. \n\nBut it's this overwhelming, crushing feeling that I absolutely have to do it. Like there's an actual physical weight pushing me back down into my desk chair. If I stand up, grab my purse, and try to walk toward the elevator, my heart just starts pounding out of my chest, and my brain is screaming at me that if I don't do it just one more time, something terrible is going to happen and it will be completely my fault. So I feel forced by that fear, if that makes sense. \n\nBut then... when I do sit back down, and I pull the file back up, and I run the numbers again, tracing my finger down the monitor to make sure every single decimal point is exactly where it should be... I do get this tiny, split-second of relief. It's like, \"Okay, thank God, it's fine, I didn't ruin anyone's life today.\" My shoulders drop, I can finally take a real breath. \n\nThe problem is, that feeling just doesn't last. It evaporates almost instantly. Before I can even get my coat on, the doubt creeps back in. My brain says, \"Wait, did you actually check the third tab, or did you just skim it?\" And then I'm forced to sit down and do it all over again. So I guess I'm chasing that relief, but I only have to chase it because I feel completely held hostage by the thought that if I don't check, everything falls apart and it's on my shoulders. That's why I'm staying at work for hours after everyone leaves, even when I already checked everything twenty times. I'm just trapped in this cycle of being terrified, checking, feeling okay for two seconds, and then being terrified all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers or the way the spreadsheets are organized need to be a certain way or look a certain way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Um, like... organized a certain way? Or looking a certain way? No, not really. I mean, the spreadsheets we use are mostly just standard templates anyway. The software we use just exports everything into these basic, you know, standard Excel grids, and I really don't care about the formatting at all. Like, I don't care if the columns are perfectly spaced out, or if the font is Arial instead of Calibri, or if the headers are highlighted in yellow or something. I know some of the other clerks at the office get really particular about that stuff, like they want everything to look super neat and color-coded and perfectly aligned before they save the file, but that doesn't bother me even a little bit. \n\nFor me, it is one hundred percent just about the actual data. It's not about how the numbers look on the screen, it's about what they *mean*. Like I was saying before, my whole thing is just... is the math right? Did I type a five instead of a two? Did I put a decimal in the wrong spot? Because that's the stuff that actually ruins things. A messy spreadsheet isn't going to cause the IRS to audit us, but a wrong number absolutely will. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm not sitting there for hours in the dark rearranging the rows to make them look pretty or balanced or whatever. I'm just staring at the exact same cell, reading the value over and over and over again, terrified that my eyes are playing tricks on me and I missed a negative sign. If I knew for an absolute fact that the math was perfect, it could be the ugliest, most disorganized spreadsheet in the entire world and I wouldn't care at all. It's just that fear of making a fatal mistake that I can't let go of, you know? It always just comes back to whether or not I'm going to be the reason someone's paycheck bounces.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Um... yeah. Yeah, it really has. I mean, my family, they mostly just get frustrated that I'm never home for dinner anymore, but with my friends... it's been really, really bad. 

Like, for example, I've completely stopped making it to my choir rehearsals. I've been in this community choir for, gosh, since I graduated high school, so like five years now? And we usually practice twice a week, and it’s always been the highlight of my week, you know? Seeing everybody, catching up, singing... it was my main social outlet. All my closest friends are in it. But because I just literally can't get out of the office on time... I mean, rehearsal starts at 6:30, and I'm still sitting at my desk at 7:00, 7:30, just paralyzed, checking those same columns over and over again. So I finally just had to stop going entirely. It was just too awful texting my friend Sarah every single week with some excuse about being swamped. 

And then there's the youth center. I had all these commitments there, helping run the after-school programs, which I really love doing, but I've stopped making it to those, too. The other volunteers were counting on me, and I just... I just haven't been there. I can't get there. I'm trapped in that dark office, and it makes me feel like such a terrible friend, such a horrible person for letting everyone down.

And you know what the crazy part is? The absolute craziest part is that all this checking is supposed to protect my job and keep things running smoothly, right? But it’s actually doing the exact opposite. Because I was spending so much time going back and re-verifying old data, I actually missed a filing deadline. A real, actual deadline. I was so busy checking things that were already done that the new work didn't get turned in. I got called into a formal meeting at work about it. My manager called me in, closed the door and everything, and they were asking me how I could miss something so important when they see my car in the parking lot until nine o'clock every night. 

And I... I just sat there. I didn't know what to say. I couldn't exactly tell them, "Oh, I'm spending four hours a night making sure I didn't accidentally type a three instead of an eight on a form from last month." It was so humiliating. It's just ruining everything. My friends at choir, the youth center, my actual job... I'm just watching it all fall apart because I can't leave the office, and I still can't stop checking. I just can't stop.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you for seeing me. I... well, I'm here because things at work have just gotten completely out of hand, and I don't know what else to do. I work as a bookkeeping clerk, right? So I deal with numbers and spreadsheets all day. And lately—well, honestly it's been building up for a while, but it's just so bad now—I can't stop going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations again and again. \n\nI mean, I'll finish a ledger, and I know I did it right. I'll even check the math, and it balances perfectly. But then this feeling hits me, just this overwhelming dread, and I have to go back and check it again. And then I'll check it again. And I'll think, \"Okay, that's the last time, I'm going to close the file,\" but my hand just won't click the mouse to close it, because my brain is screaming at me that if I don't check it one more time, I've missed a decimal point or transposed a number, and the whole company's finances are going to be ruined, and it'll be entirely my fault. \n\nIt's gotten to the point where I'm staying at work for hours and hours after everyone else leaves. Like, the office is dark, the cleaning crew has already come and gone, and I'm just sitting there at my desk, staring at a screen I've already reviewed twenty times. I just... I feel like something terrible is going to happen if I don't do it just one more time, and I can't stop myself, no matter how exhausted I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating these checking behaviors outside of your work, like with personal finances or other tasks at home?\n\nPatient: I mean, outside of work? Um... no, not really. It's funny, you'd think I would, right? Like if I'm so obsessed with the numbers at work, you'd think I'd be exactly the same way with my own bank account or my bills at home. But honestly, I just... I don't. When it comes to my own stuff, I just pay my electric bill or whatever and I don't even think twice about it. I might glance at my bank balance on my phone, but I'm not sitting there for three hours making sure the math adds up. \n\nIt's really just the work stuff. And I've thought about that a lot, actually. Like, why is it only at the office? I think maybe it's because... well, at home, if I mess up my own checking account, it's just me, you know? Like, I bounce a check, I get a fee, whatever. It's my problem. But at work, I'm handling thousands and thousands of dollars that belong to the company, and we have vendors relying on us, and payroll, and if I mess that up, it's not just a little overdraft fee. It's like... people's livelihoods. And that weight, that responsibility, it just sits on my chest all day. So when I go home, I'm just so mentally drained from checking the work spreadsheets that I don't even have the energy to care about my own mail. I just leave it on the kitchen counter and go to sleep. So, yeah, no, it doesn't really happen at home. Just work. Just... constantly at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts or ideas that bother you outside of your concerns about work and the checking behaviors you've described?\n\nPatient: Um, outside of work? Not really, no. Like I said, it really is just... it all comes back to the office. But even when I'm not physically sitting there doing the checking, the thoughts about it just... they don't stop. I mean, the ideas that pop into my head are just so loud, and they follow me everywhere. \n\nSo, for example, I'll be driving home, or I'll be sitting on my couch trying to watch a show, and even after I *know* the numbers match—like, I literally saw the zero balance, I saw the green checkmark in the software, I know I did the math right—this thought just keeps jumping in. It just completely invades my head that I missed one hidden mistake somewhere deep in the payroll file, or maybe in the quarterly taxes. \n\nAnd it's not just a passing \"oh, I hope I did that right\" kind of thought. It's this whole terrifying scenario playing out. I start picturing that somebody's check won't go through because of me. Like, maybe one of the warehouse guys won't be able to pay his rent because I transposed a digit on his direct deposit, or the firm will get in massive trouble with the IRS over a tax error and we'll get audited and it will all trace back to my desk and I'll be the reason the whole business goes under. \n\nI try so, so hard to push it away. I'll literally shake my head, or I'll turn up the radio in my car to drown it out, and I'll tell myself, \"You checked it, you checked it twenty times, it's fine.\" But it comes right back. It comes right back every single time, usually worse than before. It's like... it's like I can't trust my own eyes anymore. I'll remember seeing the right number on the screen, but my brain tells me, \"Are you sure? Are you really sure you saw a five and not a six?\" And then I'm just spiraling again, thinking about how I'm going to ruin someone's life over a payroll glitch. \n\nSo, yeah, the thoughts are just... they're completely consumed by that. I don't really have space in my brain to worry about anything else, like getting sick or whatever, because that one fear about the accounts is just taking up all the room, if that makes sense. It's just exhausting. I can't even enjoy a normal evening at home without thinking about tax penalties and ruined paychecks.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the spreadsheets again and again is what prevents the bad things you worry about, like the company's finances being ruined, from actually happening?\n\nPatient: Yes! Exactly. That is exactly what it feels like. It’s like... if I don't do it, if I just pack up my bag and walk out the door at five o'clock like a normal person, then whatever disaster is waiting to happen is going to happen, and it will be entirely my fault because I didn't stop it. \n\nI know I brought this up when we first started talking, but that's honestly the whole reason I'm here. I literally cannot stop going back over the exact same spreadsheets and the exact same calculations again and again. I'm staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves, just sitting there by myself in the dark office, and it's because checking the numbers is the only thing that feels like it's keeping the company safe. \n\nIf I just look at it one more time, I can prevent the ruined paychecks. I can prevent the tax audit. But if I don't... if I try to just close the Excel file and go home, this massive wave of panic hits me, telling me that because I didn't check it that one last time, the fatal mistake is in there. By checking it, it feels like I'm actively stopping the bad things from happening. It’s almost like it resets the danger or something, you know? Like, \"Okay, I checked it, the math is right, we're safe.\" \n\nBut then five minutes later, my brain says, \"Are you sure? What if you looked away for a second and missed a negative sign?\" And then I have to do it all over again to prevent the disaster all over again. It’s just this endless, exhausting loop where checking is the only way to protect everyone from my own potential mistakes. Even when I've already checked them twenty times, if I don't do it just one more time... I just feel like something terrible will happen, the whole place will go under, and it will be one hundred percent my fault. I just can't live with that kind of guilt, so I keep checking. I just keep checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check the spreadsheets again and again because you feel forced to, or is it more because you're trying to achieve a certain feeling, like relief?\n\nPatient: I think... I mean, it's really a bit of both? But it definitely feels like I'm forced. Like I was just saying a second ago, the whole reason I even came to this clinic in the first place is because I literally cannot stop going back over those same exact spreadsheets and calculations again and again. It's not like I want to be doing it, you know? I don't want to be sitting there at eight o'clock at night when my shift ended at five. \n\nBut it's this overwhelming, crushing feeling that I absolutely have to do it. Like there's an actual physical weight pushing me back down into my desk chair. If I stand up, grab my purse, and try to walk toward the elevator, my heart just starts pounding out of my chest, and my brain is screaming at me that if I don't do it just one more time, something terrible is going to happen and it will be completely my fault. So I feel forced by that fear, if that makes sense. \n\nBut then... when I do sit back down, and I pull the file back up, and I run the numbers again, tracing my finger down the monitor to make sure every single decimal point is exactly where it should be... I do get this tiny, split-second of relief. It's like, \"Okay, thank God, it's fine, I didn't ruin anyone's life today.\" My shoulders drop, I can finally take a real breath. \n\nThe problem is, that feeling just doesn't last. It evaporates almost instantly. Before I can even get my coat on, the doubt creeps back in. My brain says, \"Wait, did you actually check the third tab, or did you just skim it?\" And then I'm forced to sit down and do it all over again. So I guess I'm chasing that relief, but I only have to chase it because I feel completely held hostage by the thought that if I don't check, everything falls apart and it's on my shoulders. That's why I'm staying at work for hours after everyone leaves, even when I already checked everything twenty times. I'm just trapped in this cycle of being terrified, checking, feeling okay for two seconds, and then being terrified all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers or the way the spreadsheets are organized need to be a certain way or look a certain way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Um, like... organized a certain way? Or looking a certain way? No, not really. I mean, the spreadsheets we use are mostly just standard templates anyway. The software we use just exports everything into these basic, you know, standard Excel grids, and I really don't care about the formatting at all. Like, I don't care if the columns are perfectly spaced out, or if the font is Arial instead of Calibri, or if the headers are highlighted in yellow or something. I know some of the other clerks at the office get really particular about that stuff, like they want everything to look super neat and color-coded and perfectly aligned before they save the file, but that doesn't bother me even a little bit. \n\nFor me, it is one hundred percent just about the actual data. It's not about how the numbers look on the screen, it's about what they *mean*. Like I was saying before, my whole thing is just... is the math right? Did I type a five instead of a two? Did I put a decimal in the wrong spot? Because that's the stuff that actually ruins things. A messy spreadsheet isn't going to cause the IRS to audit us, but a wrong number absolutely will. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm not sitting there for hours in the dark rearranging the rows to make them look pretty or balanced or whatever. I'm just staring at the exact same cell, reading the value over and over and over again, terrified that my eyes are playing tricks on me and I missed a negative sign. If I knew for an absolute fact that the math was perfect, it could be the ugliest, most disorganized spreadsheet in the entire world and I wouldn't care at all. It's just that fear of making a fatal mistake that I can't let go of, you know? It always just comes back to whether or not I'm going to be the reason someone's paycheck bounces.\n\nDoctor: Has your need to repeatedly check the spreadsheets affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah. Yeah, it really has. I mean, my family, they mostly just get frustrated that I'm never home for dinner anymore, but with my friends... it's been really, really bad. \n\nLike, for example, I've completely stopped making it to my choir rehearsals. I've been in this community choir for, gosh, since I graduated high school, so like five years now? And we usually practice twice a week, and it’s always been the highlight of my week, you know? Seeing everybody, catching up, singing... it was my main social outlet. All my closest friends are in it. But because I just literally can't get out of the office on time... I mean, rehearsal starts at 6:30, and I'm still sitting at my desk at 7:00, 7:30, just paralyzed, checking those same columns over and over again. So I finally just had to stop going entirely. It was just too awful texting my friend Sarah every single week with some excuse about being swamped. \n\nAnd then there's the youth center. I had all these commitments there, helping run the after-school programs, which I really love doing, but I've stopped making it to those, too. The other volunteers were counting on me, and I just... I just haven't been there. I can't get there. I'm trapped in that dark office, and it makes me feel like such a terrible friend, such a horrible person for letting everyone down.\n\nAnd you know what the crazy part is? The absolute craziest part is that all this checking is supposed to protect my job and keep things running smoothly, right? But it’s actually doing the exact opposite. Because I was spending so much time going back and re-verifying old data, I actually missed a filing deadline. A real, actual deadline. I was so busy checking things that were already done that the new work didn't get turned in. I got called into a formal meeting at work about it. My manager called me in, closed the door and everything, and they were asking me how I could miss something so important when they see my car in the parking lot until nine o'clock every night. \n\nAnd I... I just sat there. I didn't know what to say. I couldn't exactly tell them, \"Oh, I'm spending four hours a night making sure I didn't accidentally type a three instead of an eight on a form from last month.\" It was so humiliating. And lately—well, honestly it's been building up for a while, but it's just so bad now—I can't stop going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations again and again. \n\nI mean, I'll finish a ledger, and I know I did it right. Um... no, not really. It's funny, you'd think I would, right? Like if I'm so obsessed with the numbers at work, you'd think I'd be exactly the same way with my own bank account or my bills at home. But honestly, I just... I don't. When it comes to my own stuff, I just pay my electric bill or whatever and I don't even think twice about it. I might glance at my bank balance on my phone, but I'm not sitting there for three hours making sure the math adds up. \n\nIt's really just the work stuff. I mean, the ideas that pop into my head are just so loud, and they follow me everywhere. \n\nSo, for example, I'll be driving home, or I'll be sitting on my couch trying to watch a show, and even after I *know* the numbers match—like, I literally saw the zero balance, I saw the green checkmark in the software, I know I did the math right—this thought just keeps jumping in. I'll literally shake my head, or I'll turn up the radio in my car to drown it out, and I'll tell myself, \"You checked it, you checked it twenty times, it's fine.\" But it comes right back. I'll remember seeing the right number on the screen, but my brain tells me, \"Are you sure? I'm trapped in that dark office, and it makes me feel like such a terrible friend, such a horrible person for letting everyone down.\n\nAnd you know what the crazy part is? The absolute craziest part is that all this checking is supposed to protect my job and keep things running smoothly, right? But it’s actually doing the exact opposite. Because I was spending so much time going back and re-verifying old data, I actually missed a filing deadline. A real, actual deadline. I was so busy checking things that were already done that the new work didn't get turned in. I got called into a formal meeting at work about it. My manager called me in, closed the door and everything, and they were asking me how I could miss something so important when they see my car in the parking lot until nine o'clock every night. \n\nAnd I... I just sat there. I didn't know what to say. I couldn't exactly tell them, \"Oh, I'm spending four hours a night making sure I didn't accidentally type a three instead of an eight on a form from last month.\" It was so humiliating. It's just ruining everything. My friends at choir, the youth center, my actual job... I'm just watching it all fall apart because I can't leave the office, and I still can't stop checking. I just can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to repeatedly check the spreadsheets is interfering with your ability to enjoy or participate in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I mean, like I was just pouring out a second ago, it has completely taken away everything I actually liked doing. It's exactly like you said, it's interfering with absolutely everything. \n\nLike I mentioned with the choir rehearsals... we were actually getting ready for our big seasonal showcase, and I had even been practicing this harmony part in my car for weeks. I loved it so much. But because I literally cannot get out of the office on time, I had to just drop out. I can't even listen to the songs we were practicing anymore because it just makes me want to cry, thinking about how Sarah and everyone else are there having fun and singing together, and I'm just... trapped at my desk staring at a screen. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with my youth-center commitments. I used to go there and help the kids with their homework, and it was honestly so rewarding. I really felt like I was doing something good, you know? But I've just completely stopped making it to those, too. I just stopped showing up because the clock would hit five, and then six, and then seven, and I'd still be checking the exact same columns. I can't enjoy anything anymore. Even if I did somehow force myself to leave work and go to the youth center, my brain would just be screaming about the spreadsheets the entire time anyway. \n\nSo instead I just sit there, missing out on my entire life, and it's not even helping my job! Like I was just saying, I still missed that filing deadline and got dragged into that formal meeting with my manager. It's just taking over absolutely everything. Every single part of my day is just ruined by this.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like there's a specific way the numbers or the spreadsheets need to be arranged for you to feel comfortable with them?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Wait, I think we actually just touched on this a few minutes ago, right? But yeah, like I was saying earlier, no, it really isn't about how they are arranged at all. I honestly could not care less if the columns are completely jumbled up or if one row is super wide and the next one is all squished, or if the text is wrapping weirdly. 

I know some people in the office, like this one guy I work with, he will spend literally twenty minutes just adjusting the column widths so everything fits on one printed page perfectly, and he uses all these different pastel background colors for the different departments. But for me, I don't care about any of that stuff. It is not a visual thing or an aesthetic thing for me whatsoever. If the spreadsheet is completely chaotic, like visually just a total disaster, but I know for an absolute, one hundred percent verified fact that the total at the bottom is correct and that I did not accidentally put a decimal in the wrong place, I would be completely fine with it. I would just save the file and go home. 

But the problem is, I just never feel like I know if it is correct. I just sit there staring at the exact same cell, completely terrified that I transposed a number or missed a negative sign. So it is not the arrangement or the layout. It is the content. It is the actual math. I just get so scared that I am going to ruin someone's life or get the company audited because of a stupid typo, so I check the value over and over and over. But the arrangement? No, I really do not have any rules about how it needs to look. I could work on the ugliest, most disorganized spreadsheet in the world as long as the numbers were actually accurate.

And lately—well, honestly it's been building up for a while, but it's just so bad now—I can't stop going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations again and again. \n\nI mean, I'll finish a ledger, and I know I did it right. I can't even enjoy a normal evening at home without thinking about tax penalties and ruined paychecks.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the spreadsheets again and again is what prevents the bad things you worry about, like the company's finances being ruined, from actually happening?\n\nPatient: Yes! Exactly. That is exactly what it feels like. It’s like... if I don't do it, if I just pack up my bag and walk out the door at five o'clock like a normal person, then whatever disaster is waiting to happen is going to happen, and it will be entirely my fault because I didn't stop it. \n\nI know I brought this up when we first started talking, but that's honestly the whole reason I'm here. I literally cannot stop going back over the exact same spreadsheets and the exact same calculations again and again. I'm staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves, just sitting there by myself in the dark office, and it's because checking the numbers is the only thing that feels like it's keeping the company safe. \n\nIf I just look at it one more time, I can prevent the ruined paychecks. Like, \"Okay, I checked it, the math is right, we're safe.\" \n\nBut then five minutes later, my brain says, \"Are you sure? It’s just this endless, exhausting loop where checking is the only way to protect everyone from my own potential mistakes. I just feel like something terrible will happen, the whole place will go under, and it will be one hundred percent my fault. I just can't live with that kind of guilt, so I keep checking. I just keep checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check the spreadsheets again and again because you feel forced to, or is it more because you're trying to achieve a certain feeling, like relief?\n\nPatient: I think... It's not like I want to be doing it, you know?
