Are you really sure you saw a five and not a six?\" And then I'm just spiraling again, thinking about how I'm going to ruin someone's life over a payroll glitch. \n\nSo, yeah, the thoughts are just... they're completely consumed by that. I don't really have space in my brain to worry about anything else, like getting sick or whatever, because that one fear about the accounts is just taking up all the room, if that makes sense. It's just exhausting. I can't even enjoy a normal evening at home without thinking about tax penalties and ruined paychecks.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the spreadsheets again and again is what prevents the bad things you worry about, like the company's finances being ruined, from actually happening?\n\nPatient: Yes! Exactly. That is exactly what it feels like. It’s like... if I don't do it, if I just pack up my bag and walk out the door at five o'clock like a normal person, then whatever disaster is waiting to happen is going to happen, and it will be entirely my fault because I didn't stop it. \n\nI know I brought this up when we first started talking, but that's honestly the whole reason I'm here. I literally cannot stop going back over the exact same spreadsheets and the exact same calculations again and again. I'm staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves, just sitting there by myself in the dark office, and it's because checking the numbers is the only thing that feels like it's keeping the company safe. \n\nIf I just look at it one more time, I can prevent the ruined paychecks. I can prevent the tax audit. But if I don't... if I try to just close the Excel file and go home, this massive wave of panic hits me, telling me that because I didn't check it that one last time, the fatal mistake is in there. By checking it, it feels like I'm actively stopping the bad things from happening. It’s almost like it resets the danger or something, you know? Like, \"Okay, I checked it, the math is right, we're safe.\" \n\nBut then five minutes later, my brain says, \"Are you sure? What if you looked away for a second and missed a negative sign?\" And then I have to do it all over again to prevent the disaster all over again. It’s just this endless, exhausting loop where checking is the only way to protect everyone from my own potential mistakes. Even when I've already checked them twenty times, if I don't do it just one more time... I just feel like something terrible will happen, the whole place will go under, and it will be one hundred percent my fault. I just can't live with that kind of guilt, so I keep checking. I just keep checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check the spreadsheets again and again because you feel forced to, or is it more because you're trying to achieve a certain feeling, like relief?\n\nPatient: I think... I mean, it's really a bit of both? But it definitely feels like I'm forced. Like I was just saying a second ago, the whole reason I even came to this clinic in the first place is because I literally cannot stop going back over those same exact spreadsheets and calculations again and again. It's not like I want to be doing it, you know? I don't want to be sitting there at eight o'clock at night when my shift ended at five. \n\nBut it's this overwhelming, crushing feeling that I absolutely have to do it. Like there's an actual physical weight pushing me back down into my desk chair. If I stand up, grab my purse, and try to walk toward the elevator, my heart just starts pounding out of my chest, and my brain is screaming at me that if I don't do it just one more time, something terrible is going to happen and it will be completely my fault. So I feel forced by that fear, if that makes sense. \n\nBut then... when I do sit back down, and I pull the file back up, and I run the numbers again, tracing my finger down the monitor to make sure every single decimal point is exactly where it should be... I do get this tiny, split-second of relief. It's like, \"Okay, thank God, it's fine, I didn't ruin anyone's life today.\" My shoulders drop, I can finally take a real breath. \n\nThe problem is, that feeling just doesn't last. It evaporates almost instantly. Before I can even get my coat on, the doubt creeps back in. My brain says, \"Wait, did you actually check the third tab, or did you just skim it?\" And then I'm forced to sit down and do it all over again. So I guess I'm chasing that relief, but I only have to chase it because I feel completely held hostage by the thought that if I don't check, everything falls apart and it's on my shoulders. That's why I'm staying at work for hours after everyone leaves, even when I already checked everything twenty times. I'm just trapped in this cycle of being terrified, checking, feeling okay for two seconds, and then being terrified all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers or the way the spreadsheets are organized need to be a certain way or look a certain way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Um, like... organized a certain way? Or looking a certain way? No, not really. I mean, the spreadsheets we use are mostly just standard templates anyway. The software we use just exports everything into these basic, you know, standard Excel grids, and I really don't care about the formatting at all. Like, I don't care if the columns are perfectly spaced out, or if the font is Arial instead of Calibri, or if the headers are highlighted in yellow or something. I know some of the other clerks at the office get really particular about that stuff, like they want everything to look super neat and color-coded and perfectly aligned before they save the file, but that doesn't bother me even a little bit. \n\nFor me, it is one hundred percent just about the actual data. It's not about how the numbers look on the screen, it's about what they *mean*. Like I was saying before, my whole thing is just... is the math right? Did I type a five instead of a two? Did I put a decimal in the wrong spot? Because that's the stuff that actually ruins things. A messy spreadsheet isn't going to cause the IRS to audit us, but a wrong number absolutely will. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm not sitting there for hours in the dark rearranging the rows to make them look pretty or balanced or whatever. I'm just staring at the exact same cell, reading the value over and over and over again, terrified that my eyes are playing tricks on me and I missed a negative sign. If I knew for an absolute fact that the math was perfect, it could be the ugliest, most disorganized spreadsheet in the entire world and I wouldn't care at all. It's just that fear of making a fatal mistake that I can't let go of, you know? It always just comes back to whether or not I'm going to be the reason someone's paycheck bounces.\n\nDoctor: Has your need to repeatedly check the spreadsheets affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah. Yeah, it really has. I mean, my family, they mostly just get frustrated that I'm never home for dinner anymore, but with my friends... it's been really, really bad. \n\nLike, for example, I've completely stopped making it to my choir rehearsals. I've been in this community choir for, gosh, since I graduated high school, so like five years now? And we usually practice twice a week, and it’s always been the highlight of my week, you know? Seeing everybody, catching up, singing... it was my main social outlet. All my closest friends are in it. But because I just literally can't get out of the office on time... I mean, rehearsal starts at 6:30, and I'm still sitting at my desk at 7:00, 7:30, just paralyzed, checking those same columns over and over again. So I finally just had to stop going entirely. It was just too awful texting my friend Sarah every single week with some excuse about being swamped. \n\nAnd then there's the youth center. I had all these commitments there, helping run the after-school programs, which I really love doing, but I've stopped making it to those, too. The other volunteers were counting on me, and I just... I just haven't been there. I can't get there. I'm trapped in that dark office, and it makes me feel like such a terrible friend, such a horrible person for letting everyone down.\n\nAnd you know what the crazy part is? The absolute craziest part is that all this checking is supposed to protect my job and keep things running smoothly, right? But it’s actually doing the exact opposite. Because I was spending so much time going back and re-verifying old data, I actually missed a filing deadline. A real, actual deadline. I was so busy checking things that were already done that the new work didn't get turned in. I got called into a formal meeting at work about it. My manager called me in, closed the door and everything, and they were asking me how I could miss something so important when they see my car in the parking lot until nine o'clock every night. \n\nAnd I... I just sat there. I didn't know what to say. I couldn't exactly tell them, \"Oh, I'm spending four hours a night making sure I didn't accidentally type a three instead of an eight on a form from last month.\" It was so humiliating. It's just ruining everything. My friends at choir, the youth center, my actual job... I'm just watching it all fall apart because I can't leave the office, and I still can't stop checking. I just can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to repeatedly check the spreadsheets is interfering with your ability to enjoy or participate in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I mean, like I was just pouring out a second ago, it has completely taken away everything I actually liked doing. It's exactly like you said, it's interfering with absolutely everything. \n\nLike I mentioned with the choir rehearsals... we were actually getting ready for our big seasonal showcase, and I had even been practicing this harmony part in my car for weeks. I loved it so much. But because I literally cannot get out of the office on time, I had to just drop out. I can't even listen to the songs we were practicing anymore because it just makes me want to cry, thinking about how Sarah and everyone else are there having fun and singing together, and I'm just... trapped at my desk staring at a screen. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with my youth-center commitments. I used to go there and help the kids with their homework, and it was honestly so rewarding. I really felt like I was doing something good, you know? But I've just completely stopped making it to those, too. I just stopped showing up because the clock would hit five, and then six, and then seven, and I'd still be checking the exact same columns. I can't enjoy anything anymore. Even if I did somehow force myself to leave work and go to the youth center, my brain would just be screaming about the spreadsheets the entire time anyway. \n\nSo instead I just sit there, missing out on my entire life, and it's not even helping my job! Like I was just saying, I still missed that filing deadline and got dragged into that formal meeting with my manager. So I'm losing the things I actually enjoy, like the choir and the volunteering, and I'm still failing at the job that's keeping me away from them in the first place. It's just taking over absolutely everything. Every single part of my day is just ruined by this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's a specific way the numbers or the spreadsheets need to be arranged for you to feel comfortable with them?\n\nPatient: Wait, I think we actually just touched on this a few minutes ago, right? But yeah, like I was saying earlier, no, it really isn't about how they are arranged at all. I honestly could not care less if the columns are completely jumbled up or if one row is super wide and the next one is all squished, or if the text is wrapping weirdly. \n\nI know some people in the office, like this one guy I work with, he will spend literally twenty minutes just adjusting the column widths so everything fits on one printed page perfectly, and he uses all these different pastel background colors for the different departments. But for me, I don't care about any of that stuff. It is not a visual thing or an aesthetic thing for me whatsoever. If the spreadsheet is completely chaotic, like visually just a total disaster, but I know for an absolute, one hundred percent verified fact that the total at the bottom is correct and that I did not accidentally put a decimal in the wrong place, I would be completely fine with it. I would just save the file and go home. \n\nBut the problem is, I just never feel like I know if it is correct. I just sit there staring at the exact same cell, completely terrified that I transposed a number or missed a negative sign. So it is not the arrangement or the layout. It is the content. It is the actual math. I just get so scared that I am going to ruin someone's life or get the company audited because of a stupid typo, so I check the value over and over and over. But the arrangement? No, I really do not have any rules about how it needs to look. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Symmetrical? Um, no, definitely not. I mean, I don't even really know how you would make a financial spreadsheet symmetrical, to be honest. Like, having the same amount of data on the left side of the screen as the right side? Or making sure the columns mirror each other? No, I don't care about anything like that at all. 

And as for exact... well, I mean, yes, the numbers have to be exact, but not in the way I think you're asking. Like, they don't have to be "exact" in some special, balanced, perfect way. I know some people have things about numbers, like my mom always has to have the car radio volume on an even number, or a multiple of five, because it just feels better to her. But it's nothing like that for me. The numbers don't have to feel "right" or look a certain way. 

If a vendor invoice is for, say, three hundred and forty-two dollars and nineteen cents, then the cell in the spreadsheet just has to say exactly that amount. But that's just because it's the literal, factual number, you know? It's not an aesthetic thing or a symmetry thing. It's purely just that absolute, paralyzing dread I was telling you about earlier, where I'm terrified that I accidentally typed nine-one instead of one-nine. Because even a tiny difference like that is going to mess up the entire monthly reconciliation, and then the client gets mad, and then the company looks bad, and my manager will pull me into another one of those awful closed-door meetings, and it'll all be my fault because I'm the clerk who entered it. 

So I have to check it a million times to make sure it's the exact number from the physical paper, but I don't have any weird rules about the numbers themselves needing to be symmetrical or even or matching some pattern. It's literally just about making sure I didn't make a typo.

I literally cannot stop going back over the exact same spreadsheets and the exact same calculations again and again. Like, \"Okay, I checked it, the math is right, we're safe.\" \n\nBut then five minutes later, my brain says, \"Are you sure? I just haven't been there. I can't get there. Because I was spending so much time going back and re-verifying old data, I actually missed a filing deadline. A real, actual deadline. I just can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to repeatedly check the spreadsheets is interfering with your ability to enjoy or participate in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I'm staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves, just sitting there by myself in the dark office, and it's because checking the numbers is the only thing that feels like it's keeping the company safe. \n\nIf I just look at it one more time, I can prevent the ruined paychecks. I can prevent the tax audit. But if I don't... if I try to just close the Excel file and go home, this massive wave of panic hits me, telling me that because I didn't check it that one last time, the fatal mistake is in there. By checking it, it feels like I'm actively stopping the bad things from happening. It’s almost like it resets the danger or something, you know? Like, \"Okay, I checked it, the math is right, we're safe.\" \n\nBut then five minutes later, my brain says, \"Are you sure? What if you looked away for a second and missed a negative sign?\" And then I have to do it all over again to prevent the disaster all over again. It’s just this endless, exhausting loop where checking is the only way to protect everyone from my own potential mistakes. Even when I've already checked them twenty times, if I don't do it just one more time... I just feel like something terrible will happen, the whole place will go under, and it will be one hundred percent my fault. I just can't live with that kind of guilt, so I keep checking. I just keep checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check the spreadsheets again and again because you feel forced to, or is it more because you're trying to achieve a certain feeling, like relief?\n\nPatient: I think... I mean, it's really a bit of both? But it definitely feels like I'm forced. Like I was just saying a second ago, the whole reason I even came to this clinic in the first place is because I literally cannot stop going back over those same exact spreadsheets and calculations again and again. It's not like I want to be doing it, you know? I don't want to be sitting there at eight o'clock at night when my shift ended at five. \n\nBut it's this overwhelming, crushing feeling that I absolutely have to do it. Like there's an actual physical weight pushing me back down into my desk chair. If I stand up, grab my purse, and try to walk toward the elevator, my heart just starts pounding out of my chest, and my brain is screaming at me that if I don't do it just one more time, something terrible is going to happen and it will be completely my fault. So I feel forced by that fear, if that makes sense. \n\nBut then... when I do sit back down, and I pull the file back up, and I run the numbers again, tracing my finger down the monitor to make sure every single decimal point is exactly where it should be... I do get this tiny, split-second of relief. It's like, \"Okay, thank God, it's fine, I didn't ruin anyone's life today.\" My shoulders drop, I can finally take a real breath. \n\nThe problem is, that feeling just doesn't last. It evaporates almost instantly. Before I can even get my coat on, the doubt creeps back in. My brain says, \"Wait, did you actually check the third tab, or did you just skim it?\" And then I'm forced to sit down and do it all over again. So I guess I'm chasing that relief, but I only have to chase it because I feel completely held hostage by the thought that if I don't check, everything falls apart and it's on my shoulders. That's why I'm staying at work for hours after everyone leaves, even when I already checked everything twenty times. I'm just trapped in this cycle of being terrified, checking, feeling okay for two seconds, and then being terrified all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers or the way the spreadsheets are organized need to be a certain way or look a certain way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Um, like... organized a certain way? Or looking a certain way? No, not really. I mean, the spreadsheets we use are mostly just standard templates anyway. The software we use just exports everything into these basic, you know, standard Excel grids, and I really don't care about the formatting at all. Like, I don't care if the columns are perfectly spaced out, or if the font is Arial instead of Calibri, or if the headers are highlighted in yellow or something. I know some of the other clerks at the office get really particular about that stuff, like they want everything to look super neat and color-coded and perfectly aligned before they save the file, but that doesn't bother me even a little bit. \n\nFor me, it is one hundred percent just about the actual data. It's not about how the numbers look on the screen, it's about what they *mean*. Like I was saying before, my whole thing is just... is the math right? Did I type a five instead of a two? Did I put a decimal in the wrong spot? Because that's the stuff that actually ruins things. A messy spreadsheet isn't going to cause the IRS to audit us, but a wrong number absolutely will. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm not sitting there for hours in the dark rearranging the rows to make them look pretty or balanced or whatever. I'm just staring at the exact same cell, reading the value over and over and over again, terrified that my eyes are playing tricks on me and I missed a negative sign. If I knew for an absolute fact that the math was perfect, it could be the ugliest, most disorganized spreadsheet in the entire world and I wouldn't care at all. It's just that fear of making a fatal mistake that I can't let go of, you know? It always just comes back to whether or not I'm going to be the reason someone's paycheck bounces.\n\nDoctor: Has your need to repeatedly check the spreadsheets affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah. Yeah, it really has. I mean, my family, they mostly just get frustrated that I'm never home for dinner anymore, but with my friends... it's been really, really bad. \n\nLike, for example, I've completely stopped making it to my choir rehearsals. I've been in this community choir for, gosh, since I graduated high school, so like five years now? And we usually practice twice a week, and it’s always been the highlight of my week, you know? Seeing everybody, catching up, singing... it was my main social outlet. All my closest friends are in it. But because I just literally can't get out of the office on time... I mean, rehearsal starts at 6:30, and I'm still sitting at my desk at 7:00, 7:30, just paralyzed, checking those same columns over and over again. So I finally just had to stop going entirely. It was just too awful texting my friend Sarah every single week with some excuse about being swamped. \n\nAnd then there's the youth center. I had all these commitments there, helping run the after-school programs, which I really love doing, but I've stopped making it to those, too. The other volunteers were counting on me, and I just... I just haven't been there. I can't get there. I'm trapped in that dark office, and it makes me feel like such a terrible friend, such a horrible person for letting everyone down.\n\nAnd you know what the crazy part is? The absolute craziest part is that all this checking is supposed to protect my job and keep things running smoothly, right? But it’s actually doing the exact opposite. Because I was spending so much time going back and re-verifying old data, I actually missed a filing deadline. A real, actual deadline. I was so busy checking things that were already done that the new work didn't get turned in. I got called into a formal meeting at work about it. My manager called me in, closed the door and everything, and they were asking me how I could miss something so important when they see my car in the parking lot until nine o'clock every night. \n\nAnd I... I just sat there. I didn't know what to say. I couldn't exactly tell them, \"Oh, I'm spending four hours a night making sure I didn't accidentally type a three instead of an eight on a form from last month.\" It was so humiliating. It's just ruining everything. My friends at choir, the youth center, my actual job... I'm just watching it all fall apart because I can't leave the office, and I still can't stop checking. I just can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to repeatedly check the spreadsheets is interfering with your ability to enjoy or participate in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I mean, like I was just pouring out a second ago, it has completely taken away everything I actually liked doing. It's exactly like you said, it's interfering with absolutely everything. \n\nLike I mentioned with the choir rehearsals... we were actually getting ready for our big seasonal showcase, and I had even been practicing this harmony part in my car for weeks. I loved it so much. But because I literally cannot get out of the office on time, I had to just drop out. I can't even listen to the songs we were practicing anymore because it just makes me want to cry, thinking about how Sarah and everyone else are there having fun and singing together, and I'm just... trapped at my desk staring at a screen. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with my youth-center commitments. I used to go there and help the kids with their homework, and it was honestly so rewarding. I really felt like I was doing something good, you know? But I've just completely stopped making it to those, too. I just stopped showing up because the clock would hit five, and then six, and then seven, and I'd still be checking the exact same columns. I can't enjoy anything anymore. Even if I did somehow force myself to leave work and go to the youth center, my brain would just be screaming about the spreadsheets the entire time anyway. \n\nSo instead I just sit there, missing out on my entire life, and it's not even helping my job! Like I was just saying, I still missed that filing deadline and got dragged into that formal meeting with my manager. So I'm losing the things I actually enjoy, like the choir and the volunteering, and I'm still failing at the job that's keeping me away from them in the first place. It's just taking over absolutely everything. Every single part of my day is just ruined by this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's a specific way the numbers or the spreadsheets need to be arranged for you to feel comfortable with them?\n\nPatient: Wait, I think we actually just touched on this a few minutes ago, right? But yeah, like I was saying earlier, no, it really isn't about how they are arranged at all. I honestly could not care less if the columns are completely jumbled up or if one row is super wide and the next one is all squished, or if the text is wrapping weirdly. \n\nI know some people in the office, like this one guy I work with, he will spend literally twenty minutes just adjusting the column widths so everything fits on one printed page perfectly, and he uses all these different pastel background colors for the different departments. But for me, I don't care about any of that stuff. It is not a visual thing or an aesthetic thing for me whatsoever. If the spreadsheet is completely chaotic, like visually just a total disaster, but I know for an absolute, one hundred percent verified fact that the total at the bottom is correct and that I did not accidentally put a decimal in the wrong place, I would be completely fine with it. I would just save the file and go home. \n\nBut the problem is, I just never feel like I know if it is correct. I just sit there staring at the exact same cell, completely terrified that I transposed a number or missed a negative sign. So it is not the arrangement or the layout. It is the content. It is the actual math. I just get so scared that I am going to ruin someone's life or get the company audited because of a stupid typo, so I check the value over and over and over. But the arrangement? No, I really do not have any rules about how it needs to look. I could work on the ugliest, most disorganized spreadsheet in the world as long as the numbers were actually accurate.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers in the spreadsheets need to be symmetrical or exact in any way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Symmetrical? Um, no, definitely not. I mean, I don't even really know how you would make a financial spreadsheet symmetrical, to be honest. Like, having the same amount of data on the left side of the screen as the right side? Or making sure the columns mirror each other? No, I don't care about anything like that at all. \n\nAnd as for exact... well, I mean, yes, the numbers have to be exact, but not in the way I think you're asking. Like, they don't have to be \"exact\" in some special, balanced, perfect way. I know some people have things about numbers, like my mom always has to have the car radio volume on an even number, or a multiple of five, because it just feels better to her. But it's nothing like that for me. The numbers don't have to feel \"right\" or look a certain way. \n\nIf a vendor invoice is for, say, three hundred and forty-two dollars and nineteen cents, then the cell in the spreadsheet just has to say exactly that amount. But that's just because it's the literal, factual number, you know? It's not an aesthetic thing or a symmetry thing. It's purely just that absolute, paralyzing dread I was telling you about earlier, where I'm terrified that I accidentally typed nine-one instead of one-nine. Because even a tiny difference like that is going to mess up the entire monthly reconciliation, and then the client gets mad, and then the company looks bad, and my manager will pull me into another one of those awful closed-door meetings, and it'll all be my fault because I'm the clerk who entered it. \n\nSo I have to check it a million times to make sure it's the exact number from the physical paper, but I don't have any weird rules about the numbers themselves needing to be symmetrical or even or matching some pattern. It's literally just about making sure I didn't make a typo.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, I don't know if I'd call it a specific feeling, like... I'm not trying to feel happy or calm or anything like that. I guess certainty is a better word for it, but even then, I don't know if I ever actually get to feel certain. That's the whole problem, right? Like, I'll be sitting there staring at the screen, and I'll check the invoice against the cell, and my brain will say, "Okay, it's correct." But then literally two seconds later, this voice in my head just goes, "But did you really look at it? Are you absolutely sure?" 

And then I just completely lose whatever tiny bit of certainty I had. It just vanishes. So I have to check it again. And I'll tell myself, "Okay, this time I'm going to look at every single digit, and I'm going to say it out loud so I know for a fact I saw it." So I'll whisper it to myself, like, "Four, seven, two, point, five, zero." And for a split second, I'll think, "Okay, I got it. It's fine." But then I'll think, "Wait, did I just say that out of habit, or did I actually read it off the screen?" And then I'm right back where I started! 

So, am I trying to achieve certainty? I guess so, yeah. I just want to know, for one hundred percent, absolutely sure, that I didn't mess up. I just want to be able to close the file and go home and not spend the entire evening wondering if I accidentally ruined the company's financials. But I just never get there. I never reach that point where I feel completely, totally sure. I just keep chasing it, over and over, and checking and checking, and the certainty just keeps slipping away no matter how many times I look at the exact same number. It's just exhausting, honestly. I just want to know it's right, but my brain just won't let me believe it.

Because even a tiny difference like that is going to mess up the entire monthly reconciliation, and then the client gets mad, and then the company looks bad, and my manager will pull me into another one of those awful closed-door meetings, and it'll all be my fault because I'm the clerk who entered it. \n\nSo I have to check it a million times to make sure it's the exact number from the physical paper, but I don't have any weird rules about the numbers themselves needing to be symmetrical or even or matching some pattern. I don't really have space in my brain to worry about anything else, like getting sick or whatever, because that one fear about the accounts is just taking up all the room, if that makes sense. It's just exhausting. I can't even enjoy a normal evening at home without thinking about tax penalties and ruined paychecks.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking the spreadsheets again and again is what prevents the bad things you worry about, like the company's finances being ruined, from actually happening?\n\nPatient: Yes! Exactly. That is exactly what it feels like. It’s like... if I don't do it, if I just pack up my bag and walk out the door at five o'clock like a normal person, then whatever disaster is waiting to happen is going to happen, and it will be entirely my fault because I didn't stop it. \n\nI know I brought this up when we first started talking, but that's honestly the whole reason I'm here. I literally cannot stop going back over the exact same spreadsheets and the exact same calculations again and again. I'm staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves, just sitting there by myself in the dark office, and it's because checking the numbers is the only thing that feels like it's keeping the company safe. \n\nIf I just look at it one more time, I can prevent the ruined paychecks. I can prevent the tax audit. But if I don't... if I try to just close the Excel file and go home, this massive wave of panic hits me, telling me that because I didn't check it that one last time, the fatal mistake is in there. By checking it, it feels like I'm actively stopping the bad things from happening. It’s almost like it resets the danger or something, you know? Like, \"Okay, I checked it, the math is right, we're safe.\" \n\nBut then five minutes later, my brain says, \"Are you sure? What if you looked away for a second and missed a negative sign?\" And then I have to do it all over again to prevent the disaster all over again. It’s just this endless, exhausting loop where checking is the only way to protect everyone from my own potential mistakes. Even when I've already checked them twenty times, if I don't do it just one more time... I just feel like something terrible will happen, the whole place will go under, and it will be one hundred percent my fault. I just can't live with that kind of guilt, so I keep checking. I just keep checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check the spreadsheets again and again because you feel forced to, or is it more because you're trying to achieve a certain feeling, like relief?\n\nPatient: I think... I mean, it's really a bit of both? But it definitely feels like I'm forced. Like I was just saying a second ago, the whole reason I even came to this clinic in the first place is because I literally cannot stop going back over those same exact spreadsheets and calculations again and again. It's not like I want to be doing it, you know? I don't want to be sitting there at eight o'clock at night when my shift ended at five. \n\nBut it's this overwhelming, crushing feeling that I absolutely have to do it. Like there's an actual physical weight pushing me back down into my desk chair. If I stand up, grab my purse, and try to walk toward the elevator, my heart just starts pounding out of my chest, and my brain is screaming at me that if I don't do it just one more time, something terrible is going to happen and it will be completely my fault. So I feel forced by that fear, if that makes sense. \n\nBut then... when I do sit back down, and I pull the file back up, and I run the numbers again, tracing my finger down the monitor to make sure every single decimal point is exactly where it should be... I do get this tiny, split-second of relief. It's like, \"Okay, thank God, it's fine, I didn't ruin anyone's life today.\" My shoulders drop, I can finally take a real breath. \n\nThe problem is, that feeling just doesn't last. It evaporates almost instantly. Before I can even get my coat on, the doubt creeps back in. My brain says, \"Wait, did you actually check the third tab, or did you just skim it?\" And then I'm forced to sit down and do it all over again. So I guess I'm chasing that relief, but I only have to chase it because I feel completely held hostage by the thought that if I don't check, everything falls apart and it's on my shoulders. That's why I'm staying at work for hours after everyone leaves, even when I already checked everything twenty times. I'm just trapped in this cycle of being terrified, checking, feeling okay for two seconds, and then being terrified all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers or the way the spreadsheets are organized need to be a certain way or look a certain way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Um, like... organized a certain way? Or looking a certain way? No, not really. I mean, the spreadsheets we use are mostly just standard templates anyway. The software we use just exports everything into these basic, you know, standard Excel grids, and I really don't care about the formatting at all. Like, I don't care if the columns are perfectly spaced out, or if the font is Arial instead of Calibri, or if the headers are highlighted in yellow or something. I know some of the other clerks at the office get really particular about that stuff, like they want everything to look super neat and color-coded and perfectly aligned before they save the file, but that doesn't bother me even a little bit. \n\nFor me, it is one hundred percent just about the actual data. It's not about how the numbers look on the screen, it's about what they *mean*. Like I was saying before, my whole thing is just... is the math right? Did I type a five instead of a two? Did I put a decimal in the wrong spot? Because that's the stuff that actually ruins things. A messy spreadsheet isn't going to cause the IRS to audit us, but a wrong number absolutely will. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm not sitting there for hours in the dark rearranging the rows to make them look pretty or balanced or whatever. I'm just staring at the exact same cell, reading the value over and over and over again, terrified that my eyes are playing tricks on me and I missed a negative sign. If I knew for an absolute fact that the math was perfect, it could be the ugliest, most disorganized spreadsheet in the entire world and I wouldn't care at all. It's just that fear of making a fatal mistake that I can't let go of, you know? It always just comes back to whether or not I'm going to be the reason someone's paycheck bounces.\n\nDoctor: Has your need to repeatedly check the spreadsheets affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah. Yeah, it really has. I mean, my family, they mostly just get frustrated that I'm never home for dinner anymore, but with my friends... it's been really, really bad. \n\nLike, for example, I've completely stopped making it to my choir rehearsals. I've been in this community choir for, gosh, since I graduated high school, so like five years now? And we usually practice twice a week, and it’s always been the highlight of my week, you know? Seeing everybody, catching up, singing... it was my main social outlet. All my closest friends are in it. But because I just literally can't get out of the office on time... I mean, rehearsal starts at 6:30, and I'm still sitting at my desk at 7:00, 7:30, just paralyzed, checking those same columns over and over again. So I finally just had to stop going entirely. It was just too awful texting my friend Sarah every single week with some excuse about being swamped. \n\nAnd then there's the youth center. I had all these commitments there, helping run the after-school programs, which I really love doing, but I've stopped making it to those, too. The other volunteers were counting on me, and I just... I just haven't been there. I can't get there. I'm trapped in that dark office, and it makes me feel like such a terrible friend, such a horrible person for letting everyone down.\n\nAnd you know what the crazy part is? The absolute craziest part is that all this checking is supposed to protect my job and keep things running smoothly, right? But it’s actually doing the exact opposite. Because I was spending so much time going back and re-verifying old data, I actually missed a filing deadline. A real, actual deadline. I was so busy checking things that were already done that the new work didn't get turned in. I got called into a formal meeting at work about it. My manager called me in, closed the door and everything, and they were asking me how I could miss something so important when they see my car in the parking lot until nine o'clock every night. \n\nAnd I... I just sat there. I didn't know what to say. I couldn't exactly tell them, \"Oh, I'm spending four hours a night making sure I didn't accidentally type a three instead of an eight on a form from last month.\" It was so humiliating. It's just ruining everything. My friends at choir, the youth center, my actual job... I'm just watching it all fall apart because I can't leave the office, and I still can't stop checking. I just can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to repeatedly check the spreadsheets is interfering with your ability to enjoy or participate in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I mean, like I was just pouring out a second ago, it has completely taken away everything I actually liked doing. It's exactly like you said, it's interfering with absolutely everything. \n\nLike I mentioned with the choir rehearsals... we were actually getting ready for our big seasonal showcase, and I had even been practicing this harmony part in my car for weeks. I loved it so much. But because I literally cannot get out of the office on time, I had to just drop out. I can't even listen to the songs we were practicing anymore because it just makes me want to cry, thinking about how Sarah and everyone else are there having fun and singing together, and I'm just... trapped at my desk staring at a screen. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with my youth-center commitments. I used to go there and help the kids with their homework, and it was honestly so rewarding. I really felt like I was doing something good, you know? But I've just completely stopped making it to those, too. I just stopped showing up because the clock would hit five, and then six, and then seven, and I'd still be checking the exact same columns. I can't enjoy anything anymore. Even if I did somehow force myself to leave work and go to the youth center, my brain would just be screaming about the spreadsheets the entire time anyway. \n\nSo instead I just sit there, missing out on my entire life, and it's not even helping my job! Like I was just saying, I still missed that filing deadline and got dragged into that formal meeting with my manager. So I'm losing the things I actually enjoy, like the choir and the volunteering, and I'm still failing at the job that's keeping me away from them in the first place. It's just taking over absolutely everything. Every single part of my day is just ruined by this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's a specific way the numbers or the spreadsheets need to be arranged for you to feel comfortable with them?\n\nPatient: Wait, I think we actually just touched on this a few minutes ago, right? But yeah, like I was saying earlier, no, it really isn't about how they are arranged at all. I honestly could not care less if the columns are completely jumbled up or if one row is super wide and the next one is all squished, or if the text is wrapping weirdly. \n\nI know some people in the office, like this one guy I work with, he will spend literally twenty minutes just adjusting the column widths so everything fits on one printed page perfectly, and he uses all these different pastel background colors for the different departments. But for me, I don't care about any of that stuff. It is not a visual thing or an aesthetic thing for me whatsoever. If the spreadsheet is completely chaotic, like visually just a total disaster, but I know for an absolute, one hundred percent verified fact that the total at the bottom is correct and that I did not accidentally put a decimal in the wrong place, I would be completely fine with it. I would just save the file and go home. \n\nBut the problem is, I just never feel like I know if it is correct. I just sit there staring at the exact same cell, completely terrified that I transposed a number or missed a negative sign. So it is not the arrangement or the layout. It is the content. It is the actual math. I just get so scared that I am going to ruin someone's life or get the company audited because of a stupid typo, so I check the value over and over and over. But the arrangement? No, I really do not have any rules about how it needs to look. I could work on the ugliest, most disorganized spreadsheet in the world as long as the numbers were actually accurate.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers in the spreadsheets need to be symmetrical or exact in any way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Symmetrical? Um, no, definitely not. I mean, I don't even really know how you would make a financial spreadsheet symmetrical, to be honest. Like, having the same amount of data on the left side of the screen as the right side? Or making sure the columns mirror each other? No, I don't care about anything like that at all. \n\nAnd as for exact... well, I mean, yes, the numbers have to be exact, but not in the way I think you're asking. Like, they don't have to be \"exact\" in some special, balanced, perfect way. I know some people have things about numbers, like my mom always has to have the car radio volume on an even number, or a multiple of five, because it just feels better to her. But it's nothing like that for me. The numbers don't have to feel \"right\" or look a certain way. \n\nIf a vendor invoice is for, say, three hundred and forty-two dollars and nineteen cents, then the cell in the spreadsheet just has to say exactly that amount. But that's just because it's the literal, factual number, you know? It's not an aesthetic thing or a symmetry thing. It's purely just that absolute, paralyzing dread I was telling you about earlier, where I'm terrified that I accidentally typed nine-one instead of one-nine. Because even a tiny difference like that is going to mess up the entire monthly reconciliation, and then the client gets mad, and then the company looks bad, and my manager will pull me into another one of those awful closed-door meetings, and it'll all be my fault because I'm the clerk who entered it. \n\nSo I have to check it a million times to make sure it's the exact number from the physical paper, but I don't have any weird rules about the numbers themselves needing to be symmetrical or even or matching some pattern. It's literally just about making sure I didn't make a typo.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to achieve a sense of certainty or a specific feeling when you check the spreadsheets repeatedly?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know if I'd call it a specific feeling, like... I'm not trying to feel happy or calm or anything like that. I guess certainty is a better word for it, but even then, I don't know if I ever actually get to feel certain. That's the whole problem, right? Like, I'll be sitting there staring at the screen, and I'll check the invoice against the cell, and my brain will say, \"Okay, it's correct.\" But then literally two seconds later, this voice in my head just goes, \"But did you really look at it? Are you absolutely sure?\" \n\nAnd then I just completely lose whatever tiny bit of certainty I had. It just vanishes. So I have to check it again. And I'll tell myself, \"Okay, this time I'm going to look at every single digit, and I'm going to say it out loud so I know for a fact I saw it.\" So I'll whisper it to myself, like, \"Four, seven, two, point, five, zero.\" And for a split second, I'll think, \"Okay, I got it. It's fine.\" But then I'll think, \"Wait, did I just say that out of habit, or did I actually read it off the screen?\" And then I'm right back where I started! \n\nSo, am I trying to achieve certainty? I guess so, yeah. I just want to know, for one hundred percent, absolutely sure, that I didn't mess up. I just want to be able to close the file and go home and not spend the entire evening wondering if I accidentally ruined the company's financials. But I just never get there. I never reach that point where I feel completely, totally sure. I just keep chasing it, over and over, and checking and checking, and the certainty just keeps slipping away no matter how many times I look at the exact same number. It's just exhausting, honestly. I just want to know it's right, but my brain just won't let me believe it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, God, yes. Yes, it really is. It’s... it’s basically taking over everything at this point. I mean, just at work alone, it’s become a huge issue. Just a couple of weeks ago, I had this major quarterly filing deadline. And I knew about it for a month, right? It wasn't a surprise. I had all the paperwork. But I just got so incredibly stuck on this one reconciliation sheet. I must have checked the accounts payable column for, I don't even know, four hours straight? I just kept thinking, 'If I submit this and it's wrong, the whole filing is ruined.' So I kept checking, and checking, and the deadline literally came and went. I missed a hard, non-negotiable filing deadline. My manager was furious. She actually called me into this formal, closed-door meeting, and it was just humiliating. I'm sitting there while she's lecturing me about my 'time management issues' and asking if I need remedial training on how to use Excel, when my whole problem is that I'm paying *too much* attention to the numbers, you know? But I couldn't exactly explain that to her without sounding completely unhinged. 

And because of all that, because I'm constantly terrified of making a mistake, I'm staying so late at the office every single night just trying to finish my basic tasks. I'm supposed to get off at five, but I'm routinely there until seven or eight o'clock, just staring at the screen. And because I can never get out of the office on time, my personal life is just... it's non-existent right now. I've completely stopped going to my choir rehearsals. I've been in this community choir for three years, and I sing alto, and we have our big showcase coming up, but I just had to stop going. I kept leaving them hanging because I was stuck at my desk. 

And it's the same thing with my youth-center commitments. I used to volunteer there twice a week, helping the middle schoolers with their homework and organizing activities. They really rely on me, and I love doing it, but I just can't make it anymore. I tell myself, 'Okay, I'll leave at 5:15 today no matter what,' but then 5:15 hits and I think, 'Just one more check on row 42, just to be absolutely sure,' and then suddenly it's 6:45 and the youth center is already wrapping up for the evening. 

So, yeah, it's interfering. It's ruining things, honestly. I feel like I'm letting everyone down—my boss, my choir director, the kids at the center... I just sit there while my whole life passes me by outside the office window. I don't even know how to explain to people why I'm always MIA lately, because 'I'm staring at a spreadsheet for four hours' doesn't exactly sound like a valid excuse, you know? They probably think I'm just flaking on them or that I don't care, which isn't true at all, it's just that I literally cannot pull myself away from the desk until I feel like the numbers are safe, and that takes hours.

I never reach that point where I feel completely, totally sure. I just keep chasing it, over and over, and checking and checking, and the certainty just keeps slipping away no matter how many times I look at the exact same number. It's just exhausting, honestly. I just want to know it's right, but my brain just won't let me believe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to repeatedly check the spreadsheets is significantly interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Oh, God, yes. I missed a hard, non-negotiable filing deadline. My manager was furious. She actually called me into this formal, closed-door meeting, and it was just humiliating. And I'll tell myself, \"Okay, this time I'm going to look at every single digit, and I'm going to say it out loud so I know for a fact I saw it.\" So I'll whisper it to myself, like, \"Four, seven, two, point, five, zero.\" And for a split second, I'll think, \"Okay, I got it. It's fine.\" But then I'll think, \"Wait, did I just say that out of habit, or did I actually read it off the screen?\" And then I'm right back where I started! \n\nSo, am I trying to achieve certainty? I guess so, yeah. I just want to know, for one hundred percent, absolutely sure, that I didn't mess up. I just want to be able to close the file and go home and not spend the entire evening wondering if I accidentally ruined the company's financials. But I just never get there. I never reach that point where I feel completely, totally sure. I just keep chasing it, over and over, and checking and checking, and the certainty just keeps slipping away no matter how many times I look at the exact same number. It's just exhausting, honestly. I just want to know it's right, but my brain just won't let me believe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to repeatedly check the spreadsheets is significantly interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Oh, God, yes. But I just got so incredibly stuck on this one reconciliation sheet. I must have checked the accounts payable column for, I don't even know, four hours straight? I just kept thinking, 'If I submit this and it's wrong, the whole filing is ruined.' So I kept checking, and checking, and the deadline literally came and went. I missed a hard, non-negotiable filing deadline. My manager was furious. She actually called me into this formal, closed-door meeting, and it was just humiliating. I'm sitting there while she's lecturing me about my 'time management issues' and asking if I need remedial training on how to use Excel, when my whole problem is that I'm paying *too much* attention to the numbers, you know? But I couldn't exactly explain that to her without sounding completely unhinged. \n\nAnd because of all that, because I'm constantly terrified of making a mistake, I'm staying so late at the office every single night just trying to finish my basic tasks. And because I can never get out of the office on time, my personal life is just... it's non-existent right now. I've completely stopped going to my choir rehearsals. I've been in this community choir for three years, and I sing alto, and we have our big showcase coming up, but I just had to stop going. I kept leaving them hanging because I was stuck at my desk. \n\nAnd it's the same thing with my youth-center commitments. I used to volunteer there twice a week, helping the middle schoolers with their homework and organizing activities. They really rely on me, and I love doing it, but I just can't make it anymore. I tell myself, 'Okay, I'll leave at 5:15 today no matter what,' but then 5:15 hits and I think, 'Just one more check on row 42, just to be absolutely sure,' and then suddenly it's 6:45 and the youth center is already wrapping up for the evening. \n\nSo, yeah, it's interfering. I don't even know how to explain to people why I'm always MIA lately, because 'I'm staring at a spreadsheet for four hours' doesn't exactly sound like a valid excuse, you know? They probably think I'm just flaking on them or that I don't care, which isn't true at all, it's just that I literally cannot pull myself away from the desk until I feel like the numbers are safe, and that takes hours.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like the spreadsheets need to be perfect for you to consider them done?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Perfect? Um, I don't... I don't really know if "perfect" is the right word. Like, when you say perfect, I think of, you know, my coworker Sarah. She's another clerk in the office, and she always color-codes her spreadsheets. Like, the headers are this specific shade of pastel blue, and she uses bold fonts for the totals, and she makes sure all the column widths are exactly the same size so it looks really nice and neat when you print it out. 

That's what I think of when someone says a perfect spreadsheet. And I don't care about any of that. Honestly, my spreadsheets probably look like a total mess to anyone else. Sometimes the fonts don't even match because I'll copy and paste data from different older files or vendor emails, and one cell will be in Arial and the next one is in Calibri, or one is size ten and the other is size eleven, and I just... I don't even bother fixing it. It doesn't bother me at all. So, no, they definitely don't need to be perfect in that way. 

It's just... it's just the accuracy. It's the terror of a typo. Like I said before, it's not about making it a masterpiece, it's just about survival, really. I just need the numbers to be exactly what they are on the paper. If the paper says twelve, the screen has to say twelve. If the spreadsheet is ugly, if the formatting is completely broken and the borders are missing, I don't care, as long as it's not the wrong number. Because if it's the wrong number, then the whole reconciliation fails, and then... well, you know, I've already told you about the meetings and my manager lecturing me. 

So, yeah, I wouldn't use the word perfect. I feel like people who want things to be perfect get some kind of satisfaction out of it, right? Like they step back and say, "Wow, look at this beautiful work I did, it's flawless." I never, ever feel that way. When I finally close a file, if I ever actually manage to pull myself away and close it, I just feel... drained. I just feel this heavy, exhausting relief that maybe, hopefully, I didn't destroy the company's accounts. There's no pride in it. There's no feeling of perfection. It's just this desperate, exhausting need to not be wrong. So if you're asking if I have some kind of perfectionist thing going on, I really don't think so. It's just the fear of making a mistake. Does that make sense? Because I feel like people always confuse the two, but for me, they are completely, totally different things.

And lately—well, honestly it's been building up for a while, but it's just so bad now—I can't stop going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations again and again. \n\nI mean, I'll finish a ledger, and I know I did it right. I literally cannot stop going back over the exact same spreadsheets and the exact same calculations again and again. Like, \"Okay, I checked it, the math is right, we're safe.\" \n\nBut then five minutes later, my brain says, \"Are you sure? It’s just this endless, exhausting loop where checking is the only way to protect everyone from my own potential mistakes. Even when I've already checked them twenty times, if I don't do it just one more time... I just feel like something terrible will happen, the whole place will go under, and it will be one hundred percent my fault. I just can't live with that kind of guilt, so I keep checking. I just keep checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check the spreadsheets again and again because you feel forced to, or is it more because you're trying to achieve a certain feeling, like relief?\n\nPatient: I think... I mean, it's really a bit of both? But it definitely feels like I'm forced. Like I was just saying a second ago, the whole reason I even came to this clinic in the first place is because I literally cannot stop going back over those same exact spreadsheets and calculations again and again. It evaporates almost instantly. Before I can even get my coat on, the doubt creeps back in. My brain says, \"Wait, did you actually check the third tab, or did you just skim it?\" And then I'm forced to sit down and do it all over again. So I guess I'm chasing that relief, but I only have to chase it because I feel completely held hostage by the thought that if I don't check, everything falls apart and it's on my shoulders. That's why I'm staying at work for hours after everyone leaves, even when I already checked everything twenty times. I'm just trapped in this cycle of being terrified, checking, feeling okay for two seconds, and then being terrified all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers or the way the spreadsheets are organized need to be a certain way or look a certain way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Um, like... organized a certain way? The software we use just exports everything into these basic, you know, standard Excel grids, and I really don't care about the formatting at all. Like, I don't care if the columns are perfectly spaced out, or if the font is Arial instead of Calibri, or if the headers are highlighted in yellow or something. I know some of the other clerks at the office get really particular about that stuff, like they want everything to look super neat and color-coded and perfectly aligned before they save the file, but that doesn't bother me even a little bit. \n\nFor me, it is one hundred percent just about the actual data. I mean, rehearsal starts at 6:30, and I'm still sitting at my desk at 7:00, 7:30, just paralyzed, checking those same columns over and over again. So I finally just had to stop going entirely. I had all these commitments there, helping run the after-school programs, which I really love doing, but I've stopped making it to those, too. The other volunteers were counting on me, and I just... I just haven't been there. I can't get there. I'm trapped in that dark office, and it makes me feel like such a terrible friend, such a horrible person for letting everyone down.\n\nAnd you know what the crazy part is? I just can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to repeatedly check the spreadsheets is interfering with your ability to enjoy or participate in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I loved it so much. But because I literally cannot get out of the office on time, I had to just drop out. I used to go there and help the kids with their homework, and it was honestly so rewarding. I really felt like I was doing something good, you know? I can't enjoy anything anymore. I honestly could not care less if the columns are completely jumbled up or if one row is super wide and the next one is all squished, or if the text is wrapping weirdly. \n\nI know some people in the office, like this one guy I work with, he will spend literally twenty minutes just adjusting the column widths so everything fits on one printed page perfectly, and he uses all these different pastel background colors for the different departments. But for me, I don't care about any of that stuff. It is not a visual thing or an aesthetic thing for me whatsoever. If the spreadsheet is completely chaotic, like visually just a total disaster, but I know for an absolute, one hundred percent verified fact that the total at the bottom is correct and that I did not accidentally put a decimal in the wrong place, I would be completely fine with it. I can't even listen to the songs we were practicing anymore because it just makes me want to cry, thinking about how Sarah and everyone else are there having fun and singing together, and I'm just... trapped at my desk staring at a screen. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with my youth-center commitments. I used to go there and help the kids with their homework, and it was honestly so rewarding. I really felt like I was doing something good, you know? But I've just completely stopped making it to those, too. I just stopped showing up because the clock would hit five, and then six, and then seven, and I'd still be checking the exact same columns. I can't enjoy anything anymore. Even if I did somehow force myself to leave work and go to the youth center, my brain would just be screaming about the spreadsheets the entire time anyway. \n\nSo instead I just sit there, missing out on my entire life, and it's not even helping my job! Like I was just saying, I still missed that filing deadline and got dragged into that formal meeting with my manager. So I'm losing the things I actually enjoy, like the choir and the volunteering, and I'm still failing at the job that's keeping me away from them in the first place. It's just taking over absolutely everything. Every single part of my day is just ruined by this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's a specific way the numbers or the spreadsheets need to be arranged for you to feel comfortable with them?\n\nPatient: Wait, I think we actually just touched on this a few minutes ago, right? But yeah, like I was saying earlier, no, it really isn't about how they are arranged at all. I honestly could not care less if the columns are completely jumbled up or if one row is super wide and the next one is all squished, or if the text is wrapping weirdly. \n\nI know some people in the office, like this one guy I work with, he will spend literally twenty minutes just adjusting the column widths so everything fits on one printed page perfectly, and he uses all these different pastel background colors for the different departments. But for me, I don't care about any of that stuff. It is not a visual thing or an aesthetic thing for me whatsoever. If the spreadsheet is completely chaotic, like visually just a total disaster, but I know for an absolute, one hundred percent verified fact that the total at the bottom is correct and that I did not accidentally put a decimal in the wrong place, I would be completely fine with it. I would just save the file and go home. \n\nBut the problem is, I just never feel like I know if it is correct. I just sit there staring at the exact same cell, completely terrified that I transposed a number or missed a negative sign. So it is not the arrangement or the layout. It is the content. It is the actual math. I just get so scared that I am going to ruin someone's life or get the company audited because of a stupid typo, so I check the value over and over and over. But the arrangement? No, I really do not have any rules about how it needs to look. I could work on the ugliest, most disorganized spreadsheet in the world as long as the numbers were actually accurate.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the numbers in the spreadsheets need to be symmetrical or exact in any way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Symmetrical? Um, no, definitely not. I mean, I don't even really know how you would make a financial spreadsheet symmetrical, to be honest. Like, having the same amount of data on the left side of the screen as the right side? Or making sure the columns mirror each other? No, I don't care about anything like that at all. \n\nAnd as for exact... well, I mean, yes, the numbers have to be exact, but not in the way I think you're asking. Like, they don't have to be \"exact\" in some special, balanced, perfect way. I know some people have things about numbers, like my mom always has to have the car radio volume on an even number, or a multiple of five, because it just feels better to her. But it's nothing like that for me. The numbers don't have to feel \"right\" or look a certain way. \n\nIf a vendor invoice is for, say, three hundred and forty-two dollars and nineteen cents, then the cell in the spreadsheet just has to say exactly that amount. But that's just because it's the literal, factual number, you know? It's not an aesthetic thing or a symmetry thing. It's purely just that absolute, paralyzing dread I was telling you about earlier, where I'm terrified that I accidentally typed nine-one instead of one-nine. Because even a tiny difference like that is going to mess up the entire monthly reconciliation, and then the client gets mad, and then the company looks bad, and my manager will pull me into another one of those awful closed-door meetings, and it'll all be my fault because I'm the clerk who entered it. \n\nSo I have to check it a million times to make sure it's the exact number from the physical paper, but I don't have any weird rules about the numbers themselves needing to be symmetrical or even or matching some pattern. It's literally just about making sure I didn't make a typo.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to achieve a sense of certainty or a specific feeling when you check the spreadsheets repeatedly?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know if I'd call it a specific feeling, like... I'm not trying to feel happy or calm or anything like that. I guess certainty is a better word for it, but even then, I don't know if I ever actually get to feel certain. That's the whole problem, right? Like, I'll be sitting there staring at the screen, and I'll check the invoice against the cell, and my brain will say, \"Okay, it's correct.\" But then literally two seconds later, this voice in my head just goes, \"But did you really look at it? Are you absolutely sure?\" \n\nAnd then I just completely lose whatever tiny bit of certainty I had. It just vanishes. So I have to check it again. And I'll tell myself, \"Okay, this time I'm going to look at every single digit, and I'm going to say it out loud so I know for a fact I saw it.\" So I'll whisper it to myself, like, \"Four, seven, two, point, five, zero.\" And for a split second, I'll think, \"Okay, I got it. It's fine.\" But then I'll think, \"Wait, did I just say that out of habit, or did I actually read it off the screen?\" And then I'm right back where I started! \n\nSo, am I trying to achieve certainty? I guess so, yeah. I just want to know, for one hundred percent, absolutely sure, that I didn't mess up. I just want to be able to close the file and go home and not spend the entire evening wondering if I accidentally ruined the company's financials. But I just never get there. I never reach that point where I feel completely, totally sure. I just keep chasing it, over and over, and checking and checking, and the certainty just keeps slipping away no matter how many times I look at the exact same number. It's just exhausting, honestly. I just want to know it's right, but my brain just won't let me believe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to repeatedly check the spreadsheets is significantly interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Oh, God, yes. Yes, it really is. It’s... it’s basically taking over everything at this point. I mean, just at work alone, it’s become a huge issue. Just a couple of weeks ago, I had this major quarterly filing deadline. And I knew about it for a month, right? It wasn't a surprise. I had all the paperwork. But I just got so incredibly stuck on this one reconciliation sheet. I must have checked the accounts payable column for, I don't even know, four hours straight? I just kept thinking, 'If I submit this and it's wrong, the whole filing is ruined.' So I kept checking, and checking, and the deadline literally came and went. I missed a hard, non-negotiable filing deadline. My manager was furious. She actually called me into this formal, closed-door meeting, and it was just humiliating. I'm sitting there while she's lecturing me about my 'time management issues' and asking if I need remedial training on how to use Excel, when my whole problem is that I'm paying *too much* attention to the numbers, you know? But I couldn't exactly explain that to her without sounding completely unhinged. \n\nAnd because of all that, because I'm constantly terrified of making a mistake, I'm staying so late at the office every single night just trying to finish my basic tasks. I'm supposed to get off at five, but I'm routinely there until seven or eight o'clock, just staring at the screen. And because I can never get out of the office on time, my personal life is just... it's non-existent right now. I've completely stopped going to my choir rehearsals. I've been in this community choir for three years, and I sing alto, and we have our big showcase coming up, but I just had to stop going. I kept leaving them hanging because I was stuck at my desk. \n\nAnd it's the same thing with my youth-center commitments. I used to volunteer there twice a week, helping the middle schoolers with their homework and organizing activities. They really rely on me, and I love doing it, but I just can't make it anymore. I tell myself, 'Okay, I'll leave at 5:15 today no matter what,' but then 5:15 hits and I think, 'Just one more check on row 42, just to be absolutely sure,' and then suddenly it's 6:45 and the youth center is already wrapping up for the evening. \n\nSo, yeah, it's interfering. It's ruining things, honestly. I feel like I'm letting everyone down—my boss, my choir director, the kids at the center... I just sit there while my whole life passes me by outside the office window. I don't even know how to explain to people why I'm always MIA lately, because 'I'm staring at a spreadsheet for four hours' doesn't exactly sound like a valid excuse, you know? They probably think I'm just flaking on them or that I don't care, which isn't true at all, it's just that I literally cannot pull myself away from the desk until I feel like the numbers are safe, and that takes hours.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the spreadsheets need to be perfect for you to consider them done?\n\nPatient: Perfect? Um, I don't... I don't really know if \"perfect\" is the right word. Like, when you say perfect, I think of, you know, my coworker Sarah. She's another clerk in the office, and she always color-codes her spreadsheets. Like, the headers are this specific shade of pastel blue, and she uses bold fonts for the totals, and she makes sure all the column widths are exactly the same size so it looks really nice and neat when you print it out. \n\nThat's what I think of when someone says a perfect spreadsheet. And I don't care about any of that. Honestly, my spreadsheets probably look like a total mess to anyone else. Sometimes the fonts don't even match because I'll copy and paste data from different older files or vendor emails, and one cell will be in Arial and the next one is in Calibri, or one is size ten and the other is size eleven, and I just... I don't even bother fixing it. It doesn't bother me at all. So, no, they definitely don't need to be perfect in that way. \n\nIt's just... it's just the accuracy. It's the terror of a typo. Like I said before, it's not about making it a masterpiece, it's just about survival, really. I just need the numbers to be exactly what they are on the paper. If the paper says twelve, the screen has to say twelve. If the spreadsheet is ugly, if the formatting is completely broken and the borders are missing, I don't care, as long as it's not the wrong number. Because if it's the wrong number, then the whole reconciliation fails, and then... well, you know, I've already told you about the meetings and my manager lecturing me. \n\nSo, yeah, I wouldn't use the word perfect. I feel like people who want things to be perfect get some kind of satisfaction out of it, right? Like they step back and say, \"Wow, look at this beautiful work I did, it's flawless.\" I never, ever feel that way. When I finally close a file, if I ever actually manage to pull myself away and close it, I just feel... drained. I just feel this heavy, exhausting relief that maybe, hopefully, I didn't destroy the company's accounts. There's no pride in it. There's no feeling of perfection. It's just this desperate, exhausting need to not be wrong. So if you're asking if I have some kind of perfectionist thing going on, I really don't think so. It's just the fear of making a mistake. Does that make sense? Because I feel like people always confuse the two, but for me, they are completely, totally different things."
  }
